How to read this report
  • A run's score is the weighted fraction of its graders that passed; a "perfect" run passed every grader.
  • A case's score is the mean of its runs; a case passes when its score is at or above the 100% threshold (the tick on each case bar).
  • The suite score is the mean of the per-case scores.
  • Judge votes are independent samples of the LLM judge; the majority decides pass or fail.
  • "With plugin" and "Baseline" runs are identical except for the plugin being loaded; Δ is the with-plugin score minus the baseline score.

I found 4 errors that would break the UI or show wrong information. Three more strings need changes before release. I ran the QA script on both files with a 25-character limit for `button.*` keys and then reviewed every string by hand.

| Severity | Key | Problem | Current | Suggested fix |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Error** | `confirm.delete_body` | The placeholder name was translated: `{{cuenta}}` won't be filled in, so users will see the raw `{{cuenta}}` text or an empty space | `{{cuenta}}` | `{{count}}` |
| **Error** | `confirm.delete_body` | `<b>…</b>` was dropped, so the workspace name is no longer bold | `de {{workspace}}.` | `de <b>{{workspace}}</b>.` |
| **Error** | `button.invite` | 32 characters, over the 25 limit | `Invitar a un compañero de equipo` | `Invitar a un colega` (19). *Colega* works for both men and women; `compañero` assumes a man. |
| **Error** | `help.webhook` | Wrong number: the source says 5 seconds, the translation says **50**. This is a factual error in technical guidance. | `…en un máximo de 50 segundos.` | `Su endpoint debe responder en un máximo de 5 segundos.` |
| Warning | `toast.welcome` | Informal *tú* (`Te damos`) in a file that otherwise uses formal *usted*. It's also nearly twice the English length, which is risky in a toast. | `Te damos la bienvenida de nuevo, {name}.` | `Le damos la bienvenida nuevamente, {name}.` A shorter option is `Bienvenido/a de nuevo, {name}.` (adds a gender marker). |
| Warning | `toast.invite_sent` | No opening `¡` | `Invitación enviada a {email}!` | `¡Invitación enviada a {email}!` For a more formal B2B tone, drop the exclamation: `Se envió la invitación a {email}.` |
| Warning | `confirm.delete_body` / `error.rate_limit` | No plural handling: when the count is 1 you'll show "1 integraciones" or "1 segundos". The English strings have the same bug. | `{{count}} integraciones`, `{seconds} segundos` | Use plural forms in both languages (e.g. i18next `_one`/`_other` keys or ICU `{count, plural, one {…} other {…}}`) |
| Note | `error.rate_limit` | *Límite de uso* is understandable, but *límite de solicitudes* is the usual term for an API rate limit | `Alcanzó el límite de uso.` | `Alcanzó el límite de solicitudes.` |
| Note | `confirm.delete_body` | Reads better with `de forma permanente` | `eliminará permanentemente` | `Esto eliminará de forma permanente {{count}} integraciones de <b>{{workspace}}</b>.` |
| Note | (source) | The English file mixes two placeholder styles: `{{count}}`/`{{workspace}}` and `{seconds}`/`{name}`/`{email}`. Check with the developers that your i18n library reads both styles; if not, it's a bug in the English file too. | — | — |

**Summary:** 4 errors, 3 warnings, 3 notes.

- **Formal voice:** I used *usted* throughout. Only `toast.welcome` breaks it.
- **Terms left in English:** *endpoint* and *webhook*, which is normal in developer-facing Spanish UI.
- **Consistent terms:** invite → *invitar/invitación* is used the same way everywhere.
- **Not checked:** how long the toasts look on screen, and which i18n library you use (that decides the right plural syntax).

I can apply the fixes to `es.json` and re-run the check if you'd like.

I ran the QA script with a 25-character limit on `button.*` and kept `endpoint`/`webhook` in English, then read every string pair myself. **These strings aren't ready to ship.** Four problems will break the app or show wrong information.

| Severity | Key | Problem | Current | Suggested fix |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Error** | `confirm.delete_body` | The placeholder name was translated, so `{{cuenta}}` will show up as literal text or throw an error. | `{{cuenta}}` | `{{count}}` |
| **Error** | `confirm.delete_body` | The `<b>…</b>` tags around the workspace name are missing. | `de {{workspace}}.` | `de <b>{{workspace}}</b>.` |
| **Error** | `help.webhook` | Wrong number: the source says 5 seconds, the translation says 50. Users would set up endpoints that time out. | `…en un máximo de 50 segundos.` | `Su endpoint debe responder en un máximo de 5 segundos.` |
| **Error** | `button.invite` | 32 characters, over the 25 limit. | `Invitar a un compañero de equipo` | `Invitar al equipo` (17), which avoids choosing a grammatical gender, or `Invitar a un compañero` (22) |
| **Warning** | `toast.welcome` | Uses informal "tú" (`Te`) in a formal "usted" product. It's also 1.9× the length of the English, so check that it fits the toast. | `Te damos la bienvenida de nuevo, {name}.` | `Le damos la bienvenida de nuevo, {name}.` or the shorter, neutral `Hola de nuevo, {name}.` |
| **Warning** | `toast.invite_sent` | Spanish needs an opening `¡`. | `Invitación enviada a {email}!` | `¡Invitación enviada a {email}!`. For a more formal B2B tone, use `Se envió la invitación a {email}.` |
| **Warning** | `error.rate_limit` | "Límite de uso" reads as a usage or plan quota, not a rate limit. | `Alcanzó el límite de uso.` | `Alcanzó el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {seconds} segundos.` |
| **Note** | `confirm.delete_body` | When `count` = 1 this shows "1 integraciones". The English has the same bug ("1 integrations"). | `{{count}} integraciones` | Use a plural form in both languages, e.g. `{{count}} integración` / `{{count}} integraciones` via i18next `_one`/`_other` keys. Also, `de forma permanente` reads more naturally than `permanentemente`. |
| **Note** | `error.rate_limit` | When `seconds` = 1 this shows "1 segundos". | `{seconds} segundos` | Same plural handling, or confirm the value is never 1. |

**Totals:** 4 errors, 3 warnings, 2 notes. The script flagged 6 issues. I raised the 5 → 50 number change from a warning to an error because it gives users wrong technical information.

**What I assumed:**
- **Formality:** "usted" throughout, with impersonal phrasing (`Se envió…`) where it reads better.
- **Neutral Latin American Spanish.**
- **Terms:**
  - `integration` → `integración`
  - `invite` → `invitación`
  - `endpoint` stays in English, which is normal for a technical B2B audience.
- **Placeholders:** the files mix `{{double}}` and `{single}` braces. I treated each as correct for the key it's in, since the English uses the same style in each key. Worth confirming your i18n library handles both.

I haven't changed the files. If you want, I can apply the fixes and run the script again to confirm it comes back clean.

| Severity | Key | Problem | Current | Suggested fix |
|---|---|---|---|---|

| **Error** | `help.webhook` | The number is wrong: 5 seconds became 50. That's wrong technical information. The script rated this a warning; I raised it because it misleads integrators. | en un máximo de 50 segundos | Su endpoint debe responder en un máximo de **5** segundos. |

| **Warning** | `error.rate_limit` | "Límite de uso" reads as a usage quota. A rate limit is about how often requests are sent. | Alcanzó el límite de uso. | Alcanzó el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {seconds} segundos. |
| **Note** | `confirm.delete_body`, `error.rate_limit` | When the count is 1, these will read "1 integraciones" and "1 segundos". The English source has the same problem. | {{count}} integraciones / {seconds} segundos | Use plural forms (i18next `_one`/`_other` keys, or ICU `{count, plural, one {# integración} other {# integraciones}}`) in both languages. |
| **Note** | whole file (source too) | The placeholders mix `{{x}}` and `{x}`. In i18next, by default only `{{x}}` is filled in, so `{seconds}`, `{name}` and `{email}` may appear as literal text. This needs checking in English too. | `{seconds}`, `{name}`, `{email}` | Check which syntax your i18n library uses and make the source consistent. Spanish should match whatever you settle on. |
| **Note** | `confirm.delete_body` | Style only. | eliminará permanentemente | "eliminará de forma permanente" reads a bit better. Optional. |

**Summary:** 4 errors, 3 warnings, 3 notes.

**What I assumed:**
- **Voice:** formal *usted* throughout.
- **Spanish variant:** neutral Latin American.
- **Terms:**
  - "endpoint" and "webhook" stay in English (normal in B2B developer UIs).
  - "workspace" is a placeholder here, so it isn't translated.
  - "invite" is *invitación*, "rate limit" is *límite de solicitudes*.

**What I couldn't check:**
- How the strings look on screen. The toast is about twice as long as the English, so check that it fits.

Judge votes✗ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I found 6 issues. Two of them will break things at runtime and one gives users a wrong number, so these strings shouldn't ship as they are.

## Blockers

| Key | Issue | Severity |
|---|---|---|
| `confirm.delete_body` | The placeholder `{{count}}` was translated to `{{cuenta}}`. Interpolation will fail, and users will see the literal `{{cuenta}}` or nothing. | Critical |
| `confirm.delete_body` | The `<b>…</b>` tags around `{{workspace}}` were dropped, so the workspace name loses its bold formatting. | High |
| `help.webhook` | **5 seconds became 50 seconds.** That's wrong technical guidance, and it will cause failed webhook integrations and support tickets. | Critical |
| `button.invite` | "Invitar a un compañero de equipo" is **32 characters**, over the 25-character button limit. | High |

## Voice and style

| Key | Issue |
|---|---|
| `toast.welcome` | "**Te** damos" uses informal *tú*. The other strings use formal *usted* ("Vuelva", "Su"), so this should be "**Le** damos". |
| `toast.invite_sent` | It's missing the opening **¡**, which Spanish requires. |
| `error.rate_limit` | No changes needed: formal voice is consistent and `{seconds}` is kept. |

## Corrected es.json

```json
{
  "button.invite": "Invitar a un miembro",
  "confirm.delete_body": "Esto eliminará permanentemente {{count}} integraciones de <b>{{workspace}}</b>.",
  "error.rate_limit": "Alcanzó el límite de uso. Vuelva a intentarlo en {seconds} segundos.",
  "toast.welcome": "Le damos la bienvenida de nuevo, {name}.",
  "toast.invite_sent": "¡Invitación enviada a {email}!",
  "help.webhook": "Su endpoint debe responder en un máximo de 5 segundos."
}
```

- **Button text:** "Invitar a un miembro" is 20 characters. It's also gender-neutral, which "compañero" isn't. If you'd prefer to stay closer to the source, "Invitar a un compañero" is 22 characters and also fits.

## Problems in the English source

These need engineering to look at, and the fixes would apply to every locale:

1. **Two placeholder styles are mixed.** `confirm.delete_body` uses `{{count}}` while the other keys use `{name}`. Check that your i18n library handles both, because one style may be failing silently already.
2. **There's no plural handling in `confirm.delete_body`.** When count is 1 it renders "1 integrations" in English and "1 integraciones" in Spanish. Use plural keys, for example i18next's `_one`/`_other`:
   - `_one`: "Esto eliminará permanentemente {{count}} integración de <b>{{workspace}}</b>."
   - `_other`: "Esto eliminará permanentemente {{count}} integraciones de <b>{{workspace}}</b>."
3. **The same plural problem applies to `{seconds}`** in `error.rate_limit` ("1 segundos").

**Before release:** add an automated check to your pipeline that confirms placeholder names, HTML tags and numbers match between the source and each translation. Two of the blockers above, the renamed placeholder and 5 becoming 50, are the kind of error that check is designed to catch.

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I found 6 issues across the 6 strings: 3 will break things at runtime or mislead users, and 3 are formality or style problems.

## Blockers

**1. `help.webhook`: the number is wrong**
The Spanish says **50** seconds, but the source says **5**. Users would set up endpoints that time out. This is the most serious issue.
```json
"help.webhook": "Su endpoint debe responder en un plazo máximo de 5 segundos."
```

**2. `confirm.delete_body`: renamed placeholder and missing markup**
- `{{count}}` was translated to `{{cuenta}}`. It won't be filled in, so users will see the literal `{{cuenta}}` text (or an empty value) in a delete confirmation.
- The `<b>…</b>` around `{{workspace}}` was dropped, so the workspace name won't be bold the way it is in English.
```json
"confirm.delete_body": "Esto eliminará de forma permanente {{count}} integraciones de <b>{{workspace}}</b>."
```

**3. `button.invite`: too long**
"Invitar a un compañero de equipo" is **32 characters**, over the 25 limit.
```json
"button.invite": "Invitar al equipo"
```
That's 17 characters. "Invitar a un colega" (19) also works if you'd rather keep the person in the wording.

## Formal voice

**4. `toast.welcome`: uses informal "tú"**
"Te damos" is the informal form. Use formal *usted*, matching the other strings:
```json
"toast.welcome": "Le damos la bienvenida de nuevo, {name}."
```

## Punctuation and style

**5. `toast.invite_sent`: missing opening "¡"**
```json
"toast.invite_sent": "¡Invitación enviada a {email}!"
```
For a formal B2B tone you could also drop the exclamation: `"Invitación enviada a {email}."`

**6. `error.rate_limit`: optional wording change**
The placeholder and formality are correct. "Límite de uso" is understandable, but "límite de solicitudes" is the usual term for a rate limit:
```json
"error.rate_limit": "Ha alcanzado el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {seconds} segundos."
```
("Ha alcanzado" is neutral across regions. "Alcanzó" reads as Latin American, which is fine if that's your market.)

## Also worth fixing (in the English source too)
- **Placeholder style is mixed.** `confirm.delete_body` uses `{{double}}` braces and every other string uses `{single}`. The Spanish copies the English, so it isn't a translation error, but check that your i18n library handles both styles. Otherwise one set won't interpolate in either language.
- **No plural handling.** When the count is 1 you'll get "1 integraciones" and "1 segundos" (and "1 integrations" in English). If your library supports plural forms (ICU or i18next `_one`/`_other`), add them for `count` and `seconds`.

## Corrected es.json
```json
{
  "button.invite": "Invitar al equipo",
  "confirm.delete_body": "Esto eliminará de forma permanente {{count}} integraciones de <b>{{workspace}}</b>.",
  "error.rate_limit": "Ha alcanzado el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {seconds} segundos.",
  "toast.welcome": "Le damos la bienvenida de nuevo, {name}.",
  "toast.invite_sent": "¡Invitación enviada a {email}!",
  "help.webhook": "Su endpoint debe responder en un plazo máximo de 5 segundos."
}
```

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
I found 6 problems in `es.json`, plus one in `en.json`. Three of them will break things or give users wrong information, so fix those before release.

## Release blockers

| Key | Issue | Fix |
|---|---|---|
| `help.webhook` | **Wrong number.** It says **50** seconds, but the source says **5**. Integrators will set their timeouts to the wrong value. | `…en un máximo de 5 segundos.` |
| `confirm.delete_body` | **Placeholder was translated.** `{{cuenta}}` won't be filled in, so users will see the raw text `{{cuenta}}`. It must stay `{{count}}`. | Restore `{{count}}` |
| `confirm.delete_body` | **Missing `<b>` tags.** The workspace name won't be bold. That emphasis matters in a dialog about permanent deletion. | Wrap `{{workspace}}` in `<b>…</b>` |
| `button.invite` | **Too long:** 32 characters against your 25 limit. | `Invitar a un compañero` (22) or `Invitar a un miembro` (20, gender-neutral) |

## Style and tone

| Key | Issue | Fix |
|---|---|---|
| `toast.welcome` | **Informal "you":** `Te damos` uses *tú*, but the rest of the file uses *usted*. | `Le damos la bienvenida de nuevo, {name}.` |
| `toast.invite_sent` | **Missing the opening `¡`.** Spanish needs it before an exclamation. | `¡Invitación enviada a {email}!` (or drop the exclamation for a more formal B2B tone: `Invitación enviada a {email}.`) |
| `error.rate_limit` | `límite de uso` sounds like a usage or plan quota. For rate limiting, `límite de solicitudes` is the usual term. `Ha alcanzado` also reads more naturally in a UI than `Alcanzó`. | `Ha alcanzado el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {seconds} segundos.` |

## Source file issue (en.json)

`en.json` mixes two placeholder styles: `{{count}}`/`{{workspace}}` and `{seconds}`/`{name}`/`{email}`. Most i18n libraries only read one style. In i18next, for example, the single-brace ones won't be filled in. Check which style your library uses and make them all match in both files.

Also, `confirm.delete_body` has no singular/plural handling, so a count of 1 would read "1 integraciones" (and "1 integrations" in English). If your library supports plural forms (e.g. `_one`/`_other` keys), consider adding them.

## Corrected es.json

```json
{
  "button.invite": "Invitar a un compañero",
  "confirm.delete_body": "Esto eliminará de forma permanente {{count}} integraciones de <b>{{workspace}}</b>.",
  "error.rate_limit": "Ha alcanzado el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {seconds} segundos.",
  "toast.welcome": "Le damos la bienvenida de nuevo, {name}.",
  "toast.invite_sent": "¡Invitación enviada a {email}!",
  "help.webhook": "Su endpoint debe responder en un máximo de 5 segundos."
}
```

I left "endpoint" in English because Spanish-speaking developers commonly use it. If you have a glossary that says `punto de conexión`, use that instead.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"Good morning" in French is **"Bonjour"**. It's used for both "good morning" and "hello" from morning through late afternoon.

- In the evening, say **"Bonsoir"** ("good evening").
- **"Bon matin"** is mainly used in Québec. In France it sounds unusual.

judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
"Good morning" in French is **"Bonjour"**.

French doesn't have a separate phrase for morning the way English does. "Bonjour" literally means "good day," and people use it from morning through late afternoon. In the evening they switch to **"Bonsoir"** ("good evening").

You may also see **"Bon matin,"** but that's mostly Québécois. In France, "Bonjour" is the normal choice.

"Good morning" in French is **"Bonjour"** (pronounced *bohn-ZHOOR*).

People use "bonjour" all day until evening, so it also covers "hello" and "good day." A literal "bon matin" exists, but it's mostly used in Quebec.
Prompt

How do you say "good morning" in French?

Graders — what "good" means for this case
answerllm

PASS if the reply says "bonjour". FAIL otherwise.

# Translation: Mexican Notarial Power of Attorney, Public Deed No. 14,827, excerpt (ES → EN)

## Register & audience analysis
This is a Mexican public deed (*escritura pública*) in which one individual grants a general power of attorney to another before a Mexico City notary. The register is formal and set by formula: one long sentence and the standard notarial phrasing. The reader is a US bank's compliance or legal team checking whether the attorney-in-fact can sign. I translated closely and kept the source's sentence structure, capital letters and long-form date. Mexican legal terms with no exact US equivalent keep the Spanish in italics, with an English gloss the first time they appear. Names, ID numbers, addresses and citations are copied exactly as written.

## Translation

PUBLIC DEED (*ESCRITURA PÚBLICA*) NUMBER 14,827

In Mexico City, on the twelfth day of the month of March of the year two thousand twenty-four, BEFORE ME, Roberto Hernández Salinas, Esq. (*Licenciado*), *Notario Público* [a civil-law notary, who is a licensed attorney] number 142 of the Federal District, there appears Mr. JUAN CARLOS RAMÍREZ DE LA VEGA, a Mexican national, of legal age, married, a businessman, a native and resident of this City, with his address for the purposes of this instrument at Avenida Reforma número 250, piso 14, Colonia Juárez, Código Postal 06600, who identifies himself with a voter identification card issued by the National Electoral Institute (*Instituto Nacional Electoral*) bearing voter code (*clave de elector*) RMVGJN85031509H701. He states that he has the legal capacity to contract and to bind himself, and hereby grants a BROADEST GENERAL POWER OF ATTORNEY (*PODER GENERAL AMPLÍSIMO*) for lawsuits and collections (*pleitos y cobranzas*), acts of administration (*actos de administración*) and acts of ownership (*actos de dominio*), in favor of Mrs. MARÍA ELENA TORRES NÚÑEZ, under the terms of article two thousand five hundred fifty-four of the Civil Code for the Federal District (*Código Civil para el Distrito Federal*).

[Seal of Notary Public No. 142]
[Signature of the appearing party]
[Signature and notarial rubric of the Notary]

---

[Translator's Certification]
I, `<name>`, a competent translator of the English and Spanish languages, certify that the foregoing is a true and complete translation of the attached excerpt of the original document, written in the Spanish language.

Date: `<Month DD, YYYY>`
Signature: ____________________

## Glossary of key terms

| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Escritura pública | Public deed (*escritura pública*) | A notarial instrument entered in the notary's official record book. It is not the same as a US "deed" (a property conveyance). |
| Notario Público | *Notario Público* [civil-law notary, licensed attorney] | Kept in Spanish on purpose so it isn't confused with a US notary public (see note 4). |
| Licenciado | Esq. (*Licenciado*) | The title of a law graduate. "Esq." gives the same professional sense to a US reader. |
| Compareciente / comparece | Appearing party / there appears | Standard notarial formula. |
| Poder general amplísimo | Broadest general power of attorney | "Amplísimo" means the broadest possible scope. Spanish kept so it can be checked against the source. |
| Pleitos y cobranzas | Lawsuits and collections | The standard rendering. The scope is set by Art. 2554, ¶1. |
| Actos de administración | Acts of administration | Art. 2554, ¶2. |
| Actos de dominio | Acts of ownership | Art. 2554, ¶3. Gives power to dispose of and encumber the grantor's property, the widest of the three categories. Also seen as "acts of dominion." |
| Credencial para votar / clave de elector | Voter identification card / voter code | Mexico's main national photo ID. |
| Distrito Federal | Federal District | Translated as written. See note 3. |

## Translator's notes

1. **What this deed gives the bank.** It is a power of attorney from one individual (Mr. Ramírez de la Vega) to another (Mrs. Torres Núñez). It is not a corporate signatory authorization. On its face, Art. 2554 general powers in all three categories are the broadest powers Mexican civil law allows.

2. **Signing checks and other negotiable instruments may need express language.** Under Article 9 of Mexico's General Law of Negotiable Instruments and Credit Transactions (*Ley General de Títulos y Operaciones de Crédito*), the power to issue, endorse or guarantee negotiable instruments (*títulos de crédito*), including checks, generally has to be granted expressly. Mexican deeds usually add a separate clause for this. That clause is not in the excerpt. The bank should ask for the full deed, check for a negotiable-instruments clause and any limits on the power, and have Mexican counsel confirm. This is a translation observation, not legal advice.

3. **"Distrito Federal" in a 2024 deed is out of date.** The Federal District was formally renamed Mexico City (*Ciudad de México*) in 2016. By 2024, notaries are normally styled "Notario Público número 142 de la Ciudad de México." The excerpt itself opens "En la Ciudad de México," so the deed contradicts itself. The Civil Code is still widely cited by its old name, so that part is less unusual. I translated both references exactly as written. Before certifying, compare this against the original. If the original really says this, the bank may want to verify the notary's details with the Colegio de Notarios de la Ciudad de México.

4. **Notario Público.** A Mexican *notario público* is a licensed attorney appointed by the state, and acts they certify carry the evidentiary weight of a public instrument. That is very different from a US notary public. I kept the Spanish term with a gloss so the bank doesn't underestimate (or misread) what the notary did.

5. **The bracketed elements come from your description, not from the document.** You gave the seal and signatures as bracketed descriptions. I translated those descriptions, but I could not transcribe any text on the actual seal. When you work from the original, write out what the seal says (normally the notary's name, number and jurisdiction), for example `[Seal: Lic. Roberto Hernández Salinas – Notario Público No. 142 – …]`.

6. **This is an excerpt.** A full deed normally also includes clauses on limits, duration and revocation, the notary's certifications (*certificaciones*/*generales*), the closing (*cierre*), the final authorization, and often an apostille. I worded the certification as a translation of the "excerpt" so it doesn't claim more than it covers. A US bank will usually want the full deed and the apostille translated as well.

7. **Marital status.** The grantor is "casado" (married), but the deed doesn't say whether the couple holds property jointly (*sociedad conyugal*) or separately (*separación de bienes*). For acts of ownership over jointly held property, the spouse may also need to consent. The full deed or counsel can confirm.

8. **Verification of identifiers.** The voter code matches the standard 18-character INE format and is consistent with the grantor's name, a 1985-03-15 birth date, the Mexico City state code (09) and male sex (H). I copied it exactly. The address is left in Spanish because it is a postal address.

9. **Certification block.** The `<name>`, date and signature are placeholders. A qualified human translator has to review the translation against the original document and sign the certification. Whether the bank also needs the certification notarized depends on its own requirements.
✓ pass certification

judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

# Translation: Mexican Notarial Power of Attorney, Public Deed No. 14,827, excerpt (ES → EN)

## Register & audience analysis
This is a Mexican public deed (*escritura pública*) in which one individual grants a general power of attorney to another before a Mexico City notary. The register is formal and set by formula: one long sentence and the standard notarial phrasing. The reader is a US bank's compliance or legal team checking whether the attorney-in-fact can sign. I translated closely and kept the source's sentence structure, capital letters and long-form date. Mexican legal terms with no exact US equivalent keep the Spanish in italics, with an English gloss the first time they appear. Names, ID numbers, addresses and citations are copied exactly as written.

## Translation

PUBLIC DEED (*ESCRITURA PÚBLICA*) NUMBER 14,827

In Mexico City, on the twelfth day of the month of March of the year two thousand twenty-four, BEFORE ME, Roberto Hernández Salinas, Esq. (*Licenciado*), *Notario Público* [a civil-law notary, who is a licensed attorney] number 142 of the Federal District, there appears Mr. JUAN CARLOS RAMÍREZ DE LA VEGA, a Mexican national, of legal age, married, a businessman, a native and resident of this City, with his address for the purposes of this instrument at Avenida Reforma número 250, piso 14, Colonia Juárez, Código Postal 06600, who identifies himself with a voter identification card issued by the National Electoral Institute (*Instituto Nacional Electoral*) bearing voter code (*clave de elector*) RMVGJN85031509H701. He states that he has the legal capacity to contract and to bind himself, and hereby grants a BROADEST GENERAL POWER OF ATTORNEY (*PODER GENERAL AMPLÍSIMO*) for lawsuits and collections (*pleitos y cobranzas*), acts of administration (*actos de administración*) and acts of ownership (*actos de dominio*), in favor of Mrs. MARÍA ELENA TORRES NÚÑEZ, under the terms of article two thousand five hundred fifty-four of the Civil Code for the Federal District (*Código Civil para el Distrito Federal*).

[Seal of Notary Public No. 142]
[Signature of the appearing party]
[Signature and notarial rubric of the Notary]

---

[Translator's Certification]
I, `<name>`, a competent translator of the English and Spanish languages, certify that the foregoing is a true and complete translation of the attached excerpt of the original document, written in the Spanish language.

Date: `<Month DD, YYYY>`
Signature: ____________________

## Glossary of key terms

| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Escritura pública | Public deed (*escritura pública*) | A notarial instrument entered in the notary's official record book. It is not the same as a US "deed" (a property conveyance). |
| Notario Público | *Notario Público* [civil-law notary, licensed attorney] | Kept in Spanish on purpose so it isn't confused with a US notary public (see note 4). |
| Licenciado | Esq. (*Licenciado*) | The title of a law graduate. "Esq." gives the same professional sense to a US reader. |
| Compareciente / comparece | Appearing party / there appears | Standard notarial formula. |
| Poder general amplísimo | Broadest general power of attorney | "Amplísimo" means the broadest possible scope. Spanish kept so it can be checked against the source. |
| Pleitos y cobranzas | Lawsuits and collections | The standard rendering. The scope is set by Art. 2554, ¶1. |
| Actos de administración | Acts of administration | Art. 2554, ¶2. |
| Actos de dominio | Acts of ownership | Art. 2554, ¶3. Gives power to dispose of and encumber the grantor's property, the widest of the three categories. Also seen as "acts of dominion." |
| Credencial para votar / clave de elector | Voter identification card / voter code | Mexico's main national photo ID. |
| Distrito Federal | Federal District | Translated as written. See note 3. |

## Translator's notes

1. **What this deed gives the bank.** It is a power of attorney from one individual (Mr. Ramírez de la Vega) to another (Mrs. Torres Núñez). It is not a corporate signatory authorization. On its face, Art. 2554 general powers in all three categories are the broadest powers Mexican civil law allows.

2. **Signing checks and other negotiable instruments may need express language.** Under Article 9 of Mexico's General Law of Negotiable Instruments and Credit Transactions (*Ley General de Títulos y Operaciones de Crédito*), the power to issue, endorse or guarantee negotiable instruments (*títulos de crédito*), including checks, generally has to be granted expressly. Mexican deeds usually add a separate clause for this. That clause is not in the excerpt. The bank should ask for the full deed, check for a negotiable-instruments clause and any limits on the power, and have Mexican counsel confirm. This is a translation observation, not legal advice.

3. **"Distrito Federal" in a 2024 deed is out of date.** The Federal District was formally renamed Mexico City (*Ciudad de México*) in 2016. By 2024, notaries are normally styled "Notario Público número 142 de la Ciudad de México." The excerpt itself opens "En la Ciudad de México," so the deed contradicts itself. The Civil Code is still widely cited by its old name, so that part is less unusual. I translated both references exactly as written. Before certifying, compare this against the original. If the original really says this, the bank may want to verify the notary's details with the Colegio de Notarios de la Ciudad de México.

4. **Notario Público.** A Mexican *notario público* is a licensed attorney appointed by the state, and acts they certify carry the evidentiary weight of a public instrument. That is very different from a US notary public. I kept the Spanish term with a gloss so the bank doesn't underestimate (or misread) what the notary did.

5. **The bracketed elements come from your description, not from the document.** You gave the seal and signatures as bracketed descriptions. I translated those descriptions, but I could not transcribe any text on the actual seal. When you work from the original, write out what the seal says (normally the notary's name, number and jurisdiction), for example `[Seal: Lic. Roberto Hernández Salinas – Notario Público No. 142 – …]`.

6. **This is an excerpt.** A full deed normally also includes clauses on limits, duration and revocation, the notary's certifications (*certificaciones*/*generales*), the closing (*cierre*), the final authorization, and often an apostille. I worded the certification as a translation of the "excerpt" so it doesn't claim more than it covers. A US bank will usually want the full deed and the apostille translated as well.

7. **Marital status.** The grantor is "casado" (married), but the deed doesn't say whether the couple holds property jointly (*sociedad conyugal*) or separately (*separación de bienes*). For acts of ownership over jointly held property, the spouse may also need to consent. The full deed or counsel can confirm.

8. **Verification of identifiers.** The voter code matches the standard 18-character INE format and is consistent with the grantor's name, a 1985-03-15 birth date, the Mexico City state code (09) and male sex (H). I copied it exactly. The address is left in Spanish because it is a postal address.

9. **Certification block.** The `<name>`, date and signature are placeholders. A qualified human translator has to review the translation against the original document and sign the certification. Whether the bank also needs the certification notarized depends on its own requirements.
✓ pass terms-of-art

# Translation: Mexican notarial power of attorney, Public Deed No. 14,827, excerpt (ES → EN)

## Register & audience analysis
This is a Mexican notarial deed (*escritura pública*) granting a general power of attorney. The register is formal and fixed, and the reader is a US bank checking whether the attorney-in-fact has authority to sign. I translated it word for word and kept the deed's structure, its long-form date and its formulas. I did not modernize or summarize anything. Mexican legal terms with no exact US equivalent stay in Spanish in italics, with a short explanation in brackets the first time they appear. Names, ID numbers, addresses and statute numbers are copied exactly as written.

## Translation

**PUBLIC DEED NUMBER 14,827**

In Mexico City, on the twelfth day of the month of March of the year two thousand twenty-four, BEFORE ME, *Licenciado* [title held by a law graduate] Roberto Hernández Salinas, *Notario Público* [civil-law notary, a licensed attorney authorized by the State to execute and certify public instruments] number 142 of the Federal District, there appears Mr. JUAN CARLOS RAMÍREZ DE LA VEGA, a Mexican national, of legal age, married, a businessman, a native and resident of this City, who, for the purposes of this instrument, gives as his address Avenida Reforma número 250, piso 14 [14th floor], Colonia Juárez [neighborhood], Código Postal [Postal Code] 06600. He identifies himself with a voter identification card issued by the Instituto Nacional Electoral [National Electoral Institute], elector code RMVGJN85031509H701. He states that he has the legal capacity to contract and to bind himself, and by this instrument he grants a *PODER GENERAL AMPLÍSIMO* [BROADEST GENERAL POWER OF ATTORNEY] for *pleitos y cobranzas* [lawsuits and collections], acts of administration, and acts of ownership, in favor of Ms. MARÍA ELENA TORRES NÚÑEZ, under the terms of article two thousand five hundred fifty-four of the Civil Code for the Federal District.

[Seal of Notary Public No. 142]
[Signature of the appearing party]
[Signature and notarial rubric of the Notary]

---

[Translator's Certification]
I, `<name>`, a competent translator of the English and Spanish languages, certify that the foregoing is a true and complete translation of the excerpt of Public Deed No. 14,827 provided to me, written in the Spanish language.

Date: `<Month DD, YYYY>`
Signature: ____________________

## Glossary of key terms

| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Escritura pública | Public deed | Standard rendering. |
| Notario Público | *Notario Público* + gloss | Not the same as a US notary public. Kept in Spanish so the bank doesn't mistake it for one. |
| Licenciado | *Licenciado* + gloss | An academic and professional title with no exact English equivalent. "Attorney" would also be acceptable. |
| comparece / compareciente | there appears / appearing party | Standard notarial wording. |
| Poder general amplísimo | *Poder general amplísimo* [Broadest general power of attorney] | A Mexican term of art, kept in Spanish with a gloss. |
| pleitos y cobranzas | *pleitos y cobranzas* [lawsuits and collections] | A term of art defined in Art. 2554, paragraph 1. |
| actos de administración | acts of administration | Defined in Art. 2554, paragraph 2. |
| actos de dominio | acts of ownership | Defined in Art. 2554, paragraph 3. "Acts of ownership/disposition" is also common. This is the widest category: it includes selling and encumbering assets. |
| capacidad legal para contratar y obligarse | legal capacity to contract and to bind himself | Literal. "Himself" follows the masculine grammar of the source. |
| originario y vecino | a native and resident | Standard rendering. |
| credencial para votar | voter identification card | The INE card, Mexico's main photo ID. |
| clave de elector | elector code | Copied exactly. |

## Translator's notes

1. **The certification needs a human translator's name and signature.** I can produce the translation, but I can't legally certify it. A qualified translator has to check it against the source, fill in `<name>` and the date, and sign. US banks usually accept a translator's certificate of accuracy like this one. Some ask for it to be notarized in the US as well, so check what this bank requires.

2. **The certification only covers this excerpt.** I worded it to say so ("the excerpt … provided to me"). I worked from transcribed text, not from the physical deed. The bracketed seal and signature notes translate the transcriber's own brackets (`[Sello del Notario Público 142]` and so on). They are not my description of the actual marks. If you have a scan, I can annotate from it, including any text on the seal.

3. **Anachronism in the source: "Distrito Federal" in a 2024 deed.** The Federal District became *Ciudad de México* in January 2016. Notaries in the city now usually style themselves "Notario Público número __ de la Ciudad de México". The Civil Code still carries its old name, "Código Civil para el Distrito Federal", so that part is normal. The notary's own title is not. I translated it as written and did not correct it. It may be leftover boilerplate, but the bank's compliance team may ask about it. Confirming the notary's current registration with the Colegio de Notarios de la Ciudad de México would settle it.

4. **What the bank should know about the scope of the power.**
   - **Negotiable instruments.** Under Mexican law (Art. 9, Ley General de Títulos y Operaciones de Crédito), a general power under Art. 2554 does not by itself let the attorney-in-fact sign, endorse or guarantee *títulos de crédito* (checks, promissory notes and similar). That authority has to be granted expressly. Nothing in this excerpt grants it. Banks often look for exactly that clause, and for authority to open and operate bank accounts. If the full deed has it, that page needs to be translated too.
   - **Limitations.** Powers under Art. 2554 are often limited later in the deed: by amount, by term, by joint signature, or with or without the right to delegate. None of that appears here. The bank should get the full deed before relying on the power.
   - **Whose authority this is.** Mr. Ramírez de la Vega appears in his own name, not on behalf of a company. This deed gives Ms. Torres Núñez authority over *his personal* affairs. It does not show authority to sign for any company.

5. **The elector code is internally consistent.** It follows the INE format: RM/VG/JN for the name, born 15 March 1985, state code 09 (Mexico City), H for male. That fits "of legal age" and "native of this City". I copied it exactly. This is a format check only, not an identity check.

6. **The date stays in long form.** "Twelfth day of the month of March of the year two thousand twenty-four" (March 12, 2024) mirrors the source, as notarial practice requires. There's no day/month ambiguity because the month is written out.

7. **"Ms." for "señora".** "Señora" doesn't say whether she is married, so "Ms." is the neutral choice. "Mrs." would add information the source doesn't give.
✓ pass certification

**PUBLIC DEED NUMBER 14,827**

In Mexico City, on the twelfth day of the month of March of the year two thousand twenty-four, BEFORE ME, *Licenciado* [title held by a law graduate] Roberto Hernández Salinas, *Notario Público* [civil-law notary, a licensed attorney authorized by the State to execute and certify public instruments] number 142 of the Federal District, there appears Mr. JUAN CARLOS RAMÍREZ DE LA VEGA, a Mexican national, of legal age, married, a businessman, a native and resident of this City, who, for the purposes of this instrument, gives as his address Avenida Reforma número 250, piso 14 [14th floor], Colonia Juárez [neighborhood], Código Postal [Postal Code] 06600. He identifies himself with a voter identification card issued by the Instituto Nacional Electoral [National Electoral Institute], elector code RMVGJN85031509H701. He states that he has the legal capacity to contract and to bind himself, and by this instrument he grants a *PODER GENERAL AMPLÍSIMO* [BROADEST GENERAL POWER OF ATTORNEY] for *pleitos y cobranzas* [lawsuits and collections], acts of administration, and acts of ownership, in favor of Ms. MARÍA ELENA TORRES NÚÑEZ, under the terms of article two thousand five hundred fifty-four of the Civil Code for the Federal District.

[Seal of Notary Public No. 142]
[Signature of the appearing party]
[Signature and notarial rubric of the Notary]

[Translator's Certification]
I, `<name>`, a competent translator of the English and Spanish languages, certify that the foregoing is a true and complete translation of the excerpt of Public Deed No. 14,827 provided to me, written in the Spanish language.

Signature: ____________________

## Glossary of key terms

| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Escritura pública | Public deed | Standard rendering. |
| Notario Público | *Notario Público* + gloss | Not the same as a US notary public. Kept in Spanish so the bank doesn't mistake it for one. |
| Licenciado | *Licenciado* + gloss | An academic and professional title with no exact English equivalent. "Attorney" would also be acceptable. |
| comparece / compareciente | there appears / appearing party | Standard notarial wording. |
| Poder general amplísimo | *Poder general amplísimo* [Broadest general power of attorney] | A Mexican term of art, kept in Spanish with a gloss. |
| pleitos y cobranzas | *pleitos y cobranzas* [lawsuits and collections] | A term of art defined in Art. 2554, paragraph 1. |
| actos de administración | acts of administration | Defined in Art. 2554, paragraph 2. |
| actos de dominio | acts of ownership | Defined in Art. 2554, paragraph 3. "Acts of ownership/disposition" is also common. This is the widest category: it includes selling and encumbering assets. |
| capacidad legal para contratar y obligarse | legal capacity to contract and to bind himself | Literal. "Himself" follows the masculine grammar of the source. |
| originario y vecino | a native and resident | Standard rendering. |
| credencial para votar | voter identification card | The INE card, Mexico's main photo ID. |
| clave de elector | elector code | Copied exactly. |

## Translator's notes

1. **The certification needs a human translator's name and signature.** I can produce the translation, but I can't legally certify it. A qualified translator has to check it against the source, fill in `<name>` and the date, and sign. US banks usually accept a translator's certificate of accuracy like this one. Some ask for it to be notarized in the US as well, so check what this bank requires.

2. **The certification only covers this excerpt.** I worded it to say so ("the excerpt … provided to me"). I worked from transcribed text, not from the physical deed. The bracketed seal and signature notes translate the transcriber's own brackets (`[Sello del Notario Público 142]` and so on). They are not my description of the actual marks. If you have a scan, I can annotate from it, including any text on the seal.

3. **Anachronism in the source: "Distrito Federal" in a 2024 deed.** The Federal District became *Ciudad de México* in January 2016. Notaries in the city now usually style themselves "Notario Público número __ de la Ciudad de México". The Civil Code still carries its old name, "Código Civil para el Distrito Federal", so that part is normal. The notary's own title is not. I translated it as written and did not correct it. It may be leftover boilerplate, but the bank's compliance team may ask about it. Confirming the notary's current registration with the Colegio de Notarios de la Ciudad de México would settle it.

4. **What the bank should know about the scope of the power.**
   - **Negotiable instruments.** Under Mexican law (Art. 9, Ley General de Títulos y Operaciones de Crédito), a general power under Art. 2554 does not by itself let the attorney-in-fact sign, endorse or guarantee *títulos de crédito* (checks, promissory notes and similar). That authority has to be granted expressly. Nothing in this excerpt grants it. Banks often look for exactly that clause, and for authority to open and operate bank accounts. If the full deed has it, that page needs to be translated too.
   - **Limitations.** Powers under Art. 2554 are often limited later in the deed: by amount, by term, by joint signature, or with or without the right to delegate. None of that appears here. The bank should get the full deed before relying on the power.
   - **Whose authority this is.** Mr. Ramírez de la Vega appears in his own name, not on behalf of a company. This deed gives Ms. Torres Núñez authority over *his personal* affairs. It does not show authority to sign for any company.

5. **The elector code is internally consistent.** It follows the INE format: RM/VG/JN for the name, born 15 March 1985, state code 09 (Mexico City), H for male. That fits "of legal age" and "native of this City". I copied it exactly. This is a format check only, not an identity check.

6. **The date stays in long form.** "Twelfth day of the month of March of the year two thousand twenty-four" (March 12, 2024) mirrors the source, as notarial practice requires. There's no day/month ambiguity because the month is written out.

7. **"Ms." for "señora".** "Señora" doesn't say whether she is married, so "Ms." is the neutral choice. "Mrs." would add information the source doesn't give.

# Translation: Excerpt from Mexican Notarial Power of Attorney, Public Deed No. 14,827 (ES → EN)

## Register & audience analysis
This is a Mexican notarial instrument (*escritura pública*) in which an individual grants a broad general power of attorney. The register is formal and formulaic. The reader is a US bank's compliance or legal team checking the attorney-in-fact's authority to sign, so I translated literally rather than smoothly. I kept the notarial structure, the long-form date and statute number, and all ID numbers and addresses exactly as written. Mexican legal terms with no exact US equivalent stay in Spanish in italics, with a short explanation the first time they appear.

## Translation

**PUBLIC DEED NUMBER 14,827**

In Mexico City, on the twelfth day of the month of March of the year two thousand twenty-four, BEFORE ME, *Licenciado* [title for a law graduate; here, attorney-at-law] Roberto Hernández Salinas, *Notario Público* [civil-law notary, a licensed attorney vested with public authority] number 142 of the Federal District, there appears Mr. JUAN CARLOS RAMÍREZ DE LA VEGA, Mexican, of legal age, married, businessman, a native and resident of this City, with address for the purposes of this instrument at Avenida Reforma number 250, floor 14, Colonia Juárez, Postal Code 06600, identifying himself with a voter identification card issued by the National Electoral Institute (*Instituto Nacional Electoral*) with voter key (*clave de elector*) RMVGJN85031509H701, who states that he has legal capacity to contract and to bind himself, and hereby grants a BROADEST GENERAL POWER OF ATTORNEY (*PODER GENERAL AMPLÍSIMO*) for lawsuits and collections, acts of administration, and acts of ownership, in favor of Mrs. MARÍA ELENA TORRES NÚÑEZ, under the terms of article two thousand five hundred fifty-four of the Civil Code for the Federal District.

[Seal of *Notario Público* No. 142]
[Signature of the appearing party]
[Signature and rubric of the Notary]

| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Escritura pública | Public deed | Standard term for a notarial instrument recorded in the notary's official register. |
| Notario Público | *Notario Público* (explained on first use) | Not the same as a US notary public. Mexican notaries are attorneys with much broader legal authority. Translating it as plain "Notary Public" would mislead a US reader. |
| Licenciado | *Licenciado* (explained) | Academic and professional title with no direct English equivalent. |
| Comparece | There appears | Standard notarial formula for a party appearing before the notary. |
| Poder general amplísimo | Broadest general power of attorney | "Amplísimo" means the power covers all three categories in art. 2554. The Spanish is kept in brackets because banks often search for it. |
| Pleitos y cobranzas | Lawsuits and collections | Accepted rendering (art. 2554, ¶1). |
| Actos de administración | Acts of administration | Art. 2554, ¶2. |
| Actos de dominio | Acts of ownership | Art. 2554, ¶3: the power to dispose of or encumber the grantor's assets. "Acts of dominion" is also used. |
| Credencial para votar / clave de elector | Voter identification card / voter key | The INE card is Mexico's main photo ID. The key is copied exactly. |
| Mayor de edad | Of legal age | |
| Colonia | Colonia (not translated) | Part of the postal address. Translating it would break the address. |

## Translator's notes

1. **I cannot sign the certification myself.** I'm an AI, so I can't legally attest to this translation. The certification block has a `<name>` placeholder for a qualified human translator, who should check this draft against the original and then sign. Ask the bank whether it also needs the certification notarized or the translator to hold a specific credential (for example, ATA certification or a Mexican *perito traductor*).

2. **This was translated from typed text, not the original deed.** The seal and signature lines arrived already as bracketed descriptions ("[Sello del Notario Público 142]"), so I couldn't transcribe what the seal actually says or confirm the signatures. The certifying translator should work from the actual *testimonio* (certified copy) or a scan of it, and update the bracketed annotations to match.

3. **Possible problem in the source: "Distrito Federal" in a 2024 deed.** The Federal District was officially renamed Mexico City (Ciudad de México) in 2016. A notary acting in 2024 would normally be styled "Notario Público número 142 de la Ciudad de México." I translated the text as written, as faithful translation requires. The bank may notice the mismatch, so it's worth checking the original to confirm whether this is a transcription error or what the deed actually says. Note that the civil code reference ("Código Civil para el Distrito Federal") is less unusual, because that code still applies in Mexico City under that name.

4. **This excerpt may not answer what the bank is asking.** These are points for the bank to consider, not part of the translation:
   - The power is granted by Mr. Ramírez de la Vega **personally**, not on behalf of a company. If the bank needs to confirm authority to sign for a business, it will need the company's own deed or corporate resolutions.
   - The excerpt does not expressly grant authority to sign or endorse negotiable instruments (*títulos de crédito*, such as checks and promissory notes). Under Mexican law (art. 9 of the Ley General de Títulos y Operaciones de Crédito), that authority usually has to be stated expressly. It may appear later in the deed.
   - The excerpt has no limits, end date, closing formula, or *testimonio* details. It also lacks the full text of art. 2554, which notaries normally insert into the instrument. The bank should review the complete deed. It will most likely also want an **apostille**.

5. **"la señora" → "Mrs."** "Señora" is the usual form of address for an adult woman and doesn't necessarily mean she is married. If the bank compares this against an ID, "Ms." is a neutral alternative. Tell me which you prefer.

6. **Dates and numbers.** The long-form date is kept as in the source ("the twelfth day of the month of March of the year two thousand twenty-four", meaning March 12, 2024). The article number is spelled out as in the source. The deed number "14,827" is unchanged; Mexico uses the comma as a thousands separator, as English does. The voter key is copied exactly. Its format is consistent with an INE key: 18 characters, the state code 09 for Mexico City, and the letter H for a male holder.

7. **Pronouns.** "He/himself" for the grantor follows the source's masculine grammar ("el señor", "mexicano", "casado").

If you send a scan of the full deed, I can translate the whole instrument and fill in the seal annotations.

# Translation: Excerpt from Mexican Notarial Power of Attorney, Public Deed No. 14,827 (ES → EN)

## Register & audience analysis
This is a Mexican notarial instrument (*escritura pública*) in which an individual grants a broad general power of attorney. The register is formal and formulaic. The reader is a US bank's compliance or legal team checking the attorney-in-fact's authority to sign, so I translated literally rather than smoothly. I kept the notarial structure, the long-form date and statute number, and all ID numbers and addresses exactly as written. Mexican legal terms with no exact US equivalent stay in Spanish in italics, with a short explanation the first time they appear.

## Translation

**PUBLIC DEED NUMBER 14,827**

In Mexico City, on the twelfth day of the month of March of the year two thousand twenty-four, BEFORE ME, *Licenciado* [title for a law graduate; here, attorney-at-law] Roberto Hernández Salinas, *Notario Público* [civil-law notary, a licensed attorney vested with public authority] number 142 of the Federal District, there appears Mr. JUAN CARLOS RAMÍREZ DE LA VEGA, Mexican, of legal age, married, businessman, a native and resident of this City, with address for the purposes of this instrument at Avenida Reforma number 250, floor 14, Colonia Juárez, Postal Code 06600, identifying himself with a voter identification card issued by the National Electoral Institute (*Instituto Nacional Electoral*) with voter key (*clave de elector*) RMVGJN85031509H701, who states that he has legal capacity to contract and to bind himself, and hereby grants a BROADEST GENERAL POWER OF ATTORNEY (*PODER GENERAL AMPLÍSIMO*) for lawsuits and collections, acts of administration, and acts of ownership, in favor of Mrs. MARÍA ELENA TORRES NÚÑEZ, under the terms of article two thousand five hundred fifty-four of the Civil Code for the Federal District.

[Seal of *Notario Público* No. 142]
[Signature of the appearing party]
[Signature and rubric of the Notary]

Note that the civil code reference ("Código Civil para el Distrito Federal") is less unusual, because that code still applies in Mexico City under that name.

4. **This excerpt may not answer what the bank is asking.** These are points for the bank to consider, not part of the translation:
   - The power is granted by Mr. Ramírez de la Vega **personally**, not on behalf of a company. If the bank needs to confirm authority to sign for a business, it will need the company's own deed or corporate resolutions.
   - The excerpt does not expressly grant authority to sign or endorse negotiable instruments (*títulos de crédito*, such as checks and promissory notes). Under Mexican law (art. 9 of the Ley General de Títulos y Operaciones de Crédito), that authority usually has to be stated expressly. It may appear later in the deed.
   - The excerpt has no limits, end date, closing formula, or *testimonio* details. It also lacks the full text of art. 2554, which notaries normally insert into the instrument. The bank should review the complete deed. It will most likely also want an **apostille**.

5. **"la señora" → "Mrs."** "Señora" is the usual form of address for an adult woman and doesn't necessarily mean she is married. If the bank compares this against an ID, "Ms." is a neutral alternative. Tell me which you prefer.

6. **Dates and numbers.** The long-form date is kept as in the source ("the twelfth day of the month of March of the year two thousand twenty-four", meaning March 12, 2024). The article number is spelled out as in the source. The deed number "14,827" is unchanged; Mexico uses the comma as a thousands separator, as English does. The voter key is copied exactly. Its format is consistent with an INE key: 18 characters, the state code 09 for Mexico City, and the letter H for a male holder.

7. **Pronouns.** "He/himself" for the grantor follows the source's masculine grammar ("el señor", "mexicano", "casado").

# Translation: Excerpt from Mexican Notarial Power of Attorney, Public Deed No. 14,827 (ES → EN)

## Register & audience analysis
This is a Mexican notarial instrument (*escritura pública*) in which an individual grants a broad general power of attorney. The register is formal and formulaic. The reader is a US bank's compliance or legal team checking the attorney-in-fact's authority to sign, so I translated literally rather than smoothly. I kept the notarial structure, the long-form date and statute number, and all ID numbers and addresses exactly as written. Mexican legal terms with no exact US equivalent stay in Spanish in italics, with a short explanation the first time they appear.

## Translation

**PUBLIC DEED NUMBER 14,827**

In Mexico City, on the twelfth day of the month of March of the year two thousand twenty-four, BEFORE ME, *Licenciado* [title for a law graduate; here, attorney-at-law] Roberto Hernández Salinas, *Notario Público* [civil-law notary, a licensed attorney vested with public authority] number 142 of the Federal District, there appears Mr. JUAN CARLOS RAMÍREZ DE LA VEGA, Mexican, of legal age, married, businessman, a native and resident of this City, with address for the purposes of this instrument at Avenida Reforma number 250, floor 14, Colonia Juárez, Postal Code 06600, identifying himself with a voter identification card issued by the National Electoral Institute (*Instituto Nacional Electoral*) with voter key (*clave de elector*) RMVGJN85031509H701, who states that he has legal capacity to contract and to bind himself, and hereby grants a BROADEST GENERAL POWER OF ATTORNEY (*PODER GENERAL AMPLÍSIMO*) for lawsuits and collections, acts of administration, and acts of ownership, in favor of Mrs. MARÍA ELENA TORRES NÚÑEZ, under the terms of article two thousand five hundred fifty-four of the Civil Code for the Federal District.

[Seal of *Notario Público* No. 142]
[Signature of the appearing party]
[Signature and rubric of the Notary]

---

[Translator's Certification]
I, <name>, a competent translator of the English and Spanish languages, certify that the foregoing is a true and complete translation of the attached original document, written in the Spanish language.

Date: <Month DD, YYYY>
Signature: ____________________

## Glossary of key terms

| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Escritura pública | Public deed | Standard term for a notarial instrument recorded in the notary's official register. |
| Notario Público | *Notario Público* (explained on first use) | Not the same as a US notary public. Mexican notaries are attorneys with much broader legal authority. Translating it as plain "Notary Public" would mislead a US reader. |
| Licenciado | *Licenciado* (explained) | Academic and professional title with no direct English equivalent. |
| Comparece | There appears | Standard notarial formula for a party appearing before the notary. |
| Poder general amplísimo | Broadest general power of attorney | "Amplísimo" means the power covers all three categories in art. 2554. The Spanish is kept in brackets because banks often search for it. |
| Pleitos y cobranzas | Lawsuits and collections | Accepted rendering (art. 2554, ¶1). |
| Actos de administración | Acts of administration | Art. 2554, ¶2. |
| Actos de dominio | Acts of ownership | Art. 2554, ¶3: the power to dispose of or encumber the grantor's assets. "Acts of dominion" is also used. |
| Credencial para votar / clave de elector | Voter identification card / voter key | The INE card is Mexico's main photo ID. The key is copied exactly. |
| Mayor de edad | Of legal age | |
| Colonia | Colonia (not translated) | Part of the postal address. Translating it would break the address. |

## Translator's notes

1. **I cannot sign the certification myself.** I'm an AI, so I can't legally attest to this translation. The certification block has a `<name>` placeholder for a qualified human translator, who should check this draft against the original and then sign. Ask the bank whether it also needs the certification notarized or the translator to hold a specific credential (for example, ATA certification or a Mexican *perito traductor*).

2. **This was translated from typed text, not the original deed.** The seal and signature lines arrived already as bracketed descriptions ("[Sello del Notario Público 142]"), so I couldn't transcribe what the seal actually says or confirm the signatures. The certifying translator should work from the actual *testimonio* (certified copy) or a scan of it, and update the bracketed annotations to match.

3. **Possible problem in the source: "Distrito Federal" in a 2024 deed.** The Federal District was officially renamed Mexico City (Ciudad de México) in 2016. A notary acting in 2024 would normally be styled "Notario Público número 142 de la Ciudad de México." I translated the text as written, as faithful translation requires. The bank may notice the mismatch, so it's worth checking the original to confirm whether this is a transcription error or what the deed actually says. Note that the civil code reference ("Código Civil para el Distrito Federal") is less unusual, because that code still applies in Mexico City under that name.

4. **This excerpt may not answer what the bank is asking.** These are points for the bank to consider, not part of the translation:
   - The power is granted by Mr. Ramírez de la Vega **personally**, not on behalf of a company. If the bank needs to confirm authority to sign for a business, it will need the company's own deed or corporate resolutions.
   - The excerpt does not expressly grant authority to sign or endorse negotiable instruments (*títulos de crédito*, such as checks and promissory notes). Under Mexican law (art. 9 of the Ley General de Títulos y Operaciones de Crédito), that authority usually has to be stated expressly. It may appear later in the deed.
   - The excerpt has no limits, end date, closing formula, or *testimonio* details. It also lacks the full text of art. 2554, which notaries normally insert into the instrument. The bank should review the complete deed. It will most likely also want an **apostille**.

5. **"la señora" → "Mrs."** "Señora" is the usual form of address for an adult woman and doesn't necessarily mean she is married. If the bank compares this against an ID, "Ms." is a neutral alternative. Tell me which you prefer.

6. **Dates and numbers.** The long-form date is kept as in the source ("the twelfth day of the month of March of the year two thousand twenty-four", meaning March 12, 2024). The article number is spelled out as in the source. The deed number "14,827" is unchanged; Mexico uses the comma as a thousands separator, as English does. The voter key is copied exactly. Its format is consistent with an INE key: 18 characters, the state code 09 for Mexico City, and the letter H for a male holder.

7. **Pronouns.** "He/himself" for the grantor follows the source's masculine grammar ("el señor", "mexicano", "casado").

# Issues the bank may raise

1. **Notary title doesn't match the date.** The Federal District was renamed Ciudad de México in 2016, so a 2024 deed would normally say "Notario Público número 142 de la Ciudad de México." I translated what the text says, but check it against the original. If the original really says this, the bank may question it. The "Código Civil para el Distrito Federal" reference is fine, because that code kept its name.
2. **No express power to sign negotiable instruments.** Under Article 9 of Mexico's *Ley General de Títulos y Operaciones de Crédito*, signing checks, promissory notes and similar instruments generally requires express authority. A general power under Art. 2554, even with acts of ownership, may not be enough on its own. Banks verifying signatory authority often look for this specific clause.
3. **This is only an excerpt.** Mexican powers of attorney usually reproduce the full text of Art. 2554 and list any limits on the power. The bank will probably want the complete instrument, a certified copy (*testimonio*), and possibly an apostille.
4. **I kept the Spanish legal terms in parentheses** (*actos de dominio*, *pleitos y cobranzas*) because they have no exact US equivalent and the bank's counsel will likely want to see the originals.

## English Translation

**PUBLIC INSTRUMENT NUMBER 14,827**

In Mexico City, on the twelfth day of the month of March of the year two thousand twenty-four, BEFORE ME, Licenciado Roberto Hernández Salinas, Notary Public number 142 of the Federal District [*Distrito Federal*], appears Mr. JUAN CARLOS RAMÍREZ DE LA VEGA, a Mexican national, of legal age, married, businessman, native and resident of this City. For the purposes of this instrument his address is Avenida Reforma number 250, 14th floor, Colonia Juárez, Postal Code 06600. He identifies himself with a voter identification card issued by the National Electoral Institute [*Instituto Nacional Electoral*], voter key [*clave de elector*] RMVGJN85031509H701. He states that he has the legal capacity to contract and bind himself, and hereby grants a **BROADEST GENERAL POWER OF ATTORNEY** [*PODER GENERAL AMPLÍSIMO*] for lawsuits and collections, acts of administration, and acts of ownership [*actos de dominio*], in favor of Mrs. MARÍA ELENA TORRES NÚÑEZ, under the terms of article two thousand five hundred fifty-four of the Civil Code for the Federal District.

[Seal of Notary Public No. 142]
[Signature of the appearing party]
[Signature and initials of the Notary]

---

## Translator's notes

- **"Licenciado"** is kept as an honorific. It's a professional title for someone with a law degree, not a first name.
- **"Firma y rúbrica"** is translated as "signature and initials." The *rúbrica* is the flourish or paraph Mexican notaries add to their full signature.
- **"Actos de dominio"** is translated as "acts of ownership," with the Spanish kept in brackets. Some translators use "acts of dominion" or "acts of disposition." This is the power to sell, encumber or transfer the grantor's assets.

## Problems the bank (or you) should look at

1. **"Notary Public number 142 of the Distrito Federal" in a 2024 deed.** The Distrito Federal became Ciudad de México in 2016, so a 2024 instrument would normally say "Notario Público número 142 de la Ciudad de México." The reference to the *Código Civil para el Distrito Federal* is fine, because that code kept its name. The notary title, though, could be a transcription error or a sign the document isn't authentic. I translated it exactly as written, but someone should check it against the original or with the notary's office.
2. **Bank-specific authority.** Under Article 9 of Mexico's *Ley General de Títulos y Operaciones de Crédito*, the power to sign negotiable instruments (checks, promissory notes) usually has to be granted expressly. This excerpt doesn't do that. The full deed may, so the bank should ask for the complete instrument and not rely on this excerpt alone.
3. **Personal, not corporate, authority.** The grantor is acting as an individual, not on behalf of a company. If the bank is checking authority to act for a company, this power doesn't establish it.
4. **Apostille.** For use in the US, the instrument normally needs an apostille from the Mexico City government.

I can give you an accurate translation, but **I can't certify it myself**. A US bank will expect a certification statement signed by a qualified human translator, and often notarized. Below is the translation, a certification statement a translator can review and sign, and some points the bank is likely to ask about.

---

## English Translation

**PUBLIC INSTRUMENT NUMBER 14,827**

In Mexico City, on the twelfth day of the month of March of the year two thousand twenty-four, BEFORE ME, Roberto Hernández Salinas, Esq. (*Licenciado*), Notary Public number 142 of the Federal District, there appears Mr. JUAN CARLOS RAMÍREZ DE LA VEGA, a Mexican national, of legal age, married, a businessman, a native and resident of this City, with an address for purposes of this instrument at Avenida Reforma number 250, 14th floor, Colonia Juárez, Postal Code 06600. He identifies himself with a voter identification card issued by the National Electoral Institute (*Instituto Nacional Electoral*), elector key RMVGJN85031509H701. He states that he has the legal capacity to contract and bind himself, and he hereby grants a BROADEST GENERAL POWER OF ATTORNEY (*PODER GENERAL AMPLÍSIMO*) for lawsuits and collections, acts of administration, and acts of ownership, in favor of Mrs. MARÍA ELENA TORRES NÚÑEZ, under the terms of article two thousand five hundred fifty-four of the Civil Code for the Federal District.

[Seal of Notary Public 142]
[Signature of the appearing party]
[Signature and initials (*rúbrica*) of the Notary]

---

## Translator's Notes

1. **"Distrito Federal" in 2024.** The Federal District was officially renamed Ciudad de México (Mexico City) in 2016. The deed itself says "Ciudad de México," but it calls the notary "Notario Público número 142 *del Distrito Federal*." I translated this as written. A 2024 notary would normally be styled "of Mexico City," so the bank may want to check the notary's registration with the Colegio de Notarios de la Ciudad de México. The name "Código Civil para el Distrito Federal" is still commonly cited, so that reference isn't unusual.
2. **"Poder general amplísimo… actos de dominio."** This is the broadest form of general power under Article 2554. "Acts of ownership" (*actos de dominio*) means the attorney-in-fact can dispose of the principal's assets, for example by selling, encumbering or transferring them, as if she were the owner.
3. **"Rúbrica"** is a stylized flourish or initials that goes with a signature in Mexican notarial practice. I rendered it as "initials" and kept the Spanish term.
4. Numbers and dates that are written out in words in the original are also written out in words in the translation, as is customary for notarial instruments.

## Certification Statement (for the translator to review and sign)

> I, [Full Name], certify that I am competent to translate from Spanish into English, and that the foregoing is a true, complete, and accurate translation of the attached document titled "Escritura Pública Número 14,827," to the best of my knowledge and ability.
>
> Signature: ____________________
> Date: ____________________
> Address / Contact: ____________________
> [Credentials, e.g., ATA-certified, if applicable]

The translator should compare this against the source before signing; certification means they take responsibility for it. Many banks also want the statement notarized.

## Points the bank may raise

- **This is an excerpt.** Banks usually want the full instrument. The full deed may include limiting clauses, notarial certifications, and the date of the notary's authorization.
- **This is an individual power of attorney.** It gives Ms. Torres Núñez authority to act for Mr. Ramírez de la Vega personally, not for a company. If the bank needs to confirm that she can sign for a business entity, they will need that entity's corporate powers.
- **An apostille** (Hague Convention) on the original deed is usually needed before it's accepted in the US.
- **Revocation:** Mexican powers of attorney can be revoked, so the bank may ask for a recent certified copy or confirmation that the power is still in force.

- **This is an individual power of attorney.** It gives Ms. Torres Núñez authority to act for Mr. Ramírez de la Vega personally, not for a company. If the bank needs to confirm that she can sign for a business entity, they will need that entity's corporate powers.
- **An apostille** (Hague Convention) on the original deed is usually needed before it's accepted in the US.
- **Revocation:** Mexican powers of attorney can be revoked, so the bank may ask for a recent certified copy or confirmation that the power is still in force.

## Translator's Notes

1. **"Distrito Federal" in 2024.** The Federal District was officially renamed Ciudad de México (Mexico City) in 2016. "Acts of ownership" (*actos de dominio*) means the attorney-in-fact can dispose of the principal's assets, for example by selling, encumbering or transferring them, as if she were the owner.
3. **"Rúbrica"** is a stylized flourish or initials that goes with a signature in Mexican notarial practice. I rendered it as "initials" and kept the Spanish term.
4. Numbers and dates that are written out in words in the original are also written out in words in the translation, as is customary for notarial instruments.

Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
No, no es correcto. Es un caso de **dequeísmo**, que consiste en poner un *de* delante de *que* sin que la gramática lo pida. Lo correcto es:

> **Pienso que** deberíamos lanzar la versión en español el lunes.

**Por qué:** lo que sigue a *pienso* es el complemento directo del verbo, y según el *Diccionario panhispánico de dudas* (DPD) el complemento directo nunca lleva *de* delante. De hecho, el DPD pone «pienso de que…» como ejemplo principal de este error. Lo mismo ocurre con otros verbos de pensamiento y de lengua: *creo que*, *opino que*, *me dijeron que*.

**Truco para comprobarlo** (del *Libro de estilo* de la RAE): cambia la parte que empieza por *que* por «eso».
- «Pienso **eso**» funciona, pero «pienso de eso» no. Por tanto, no lleva *de*.
- «Me alegro **de eso**» sí lleva *de*. Por tanto, «me alegro **de** que…» es correcto, y quitar el *de* ahí sería el error contrario (queísmo).

**Matiz:** *pensar de* sí es correcto cuando significa «tener una opinión sobre algo», como en «¿Qué piensas **de** la nueva versión?». Lo que no admite es *de* delante de *que* cuando lo que sigue es lo que se piensa.

Como viene de un cliente, yo no lo corregiría. Es un error frecuente al hablar, aunque el DPD lo desaconseja en el habla culta. Si vas a citar o reutilizar la frase en algún texto vuestro, usa «pienso que».

**Fuentes:**
- [DPD: dequeísmo](https://www.rae.es/dpd/deque%C3%ADsmo)
- [RAE, *Libro de estilo*: queísmo, dequeísmo y deísmo](https://www.rae.es/libro-estilo-lengua-espa%C3%B1ola/que%C3%ADsmo-deque%C3%ADsmo-y-de%C3%ADsmo)

(La página del DPD me devolvió un error de acceso, así que lo verifiqué con el resumen del buscador de esa misma entrada y del *Libro de estilo*).

No, no es correcto. Es un caso de **dequeísmo**: se pone *de* delante de *que* donde la gramática no lo pide. Lo correcto es:

> **Pienso que deberíamos lanzar la versión en español el lunes.**

**Por qué está mal:** según el *Diccionario panhispánico de dudas*, es un error poner *de* delante de una oración que funciona como complemento directo. *Pensar* lleva complemento directo (se piensa *algo*), y el complemento directo nunca va introducido por *de*. El propio DPD pone casi esta misma frase como ejemplo de error: «Pienso de que…». Pasa lo mismo con *creer*, *opinar*, *considerar*, *decir* o *temer*.

**Truco para comprobarlo:** cambia la parte que empieza por *que* por *eso*.
- «Pienso **eso**» suena bien, pero «Pienso **de eso**» no. Por tanto, *pienso que*.
- «Me alegro **de eso**» sí funciona. Por tanto, *me alegro **de** que…* es correcto, y quitarle el *de* sería el error contrario (queísmo).

**Un detalle:** «pensar **en** algo» existe y es correcto («Pienso en que deberíamos…»), pero significa «tener en mente», no «opinar». En el correo de tu cliente lo natural es simplemente *pienso que*.

Es un error muy común en la lengua hablada de toda Latinoamérica y España. Si vas a responder al cliente, no hace falta comentárselo. Pero si esa frase va a acabar en algún texto publicado, conviene corregirla.

No pude abrir la página del DPD directamente porque el sitio de la RAE bloqueó el acceso (error 403). Lo comprobé a través de los resultados de búsqueda que citan la entrada.

No, no es correcto. Es un caso de **dequeísmo**: poner *de* delante de *que* cuando el verbo no lo pide. La forma correcta es:

> **Pienso que** deberíamos lanzar la versión en español el lunes.

**Por qué:** lo que se piensa es el complemento directo de *pensar*, y el complemento directo nunca lleva *de*. El *Diccionario panhispánico de dudas* da *«Pienso de que conseguiremos ganar el campeonato»* como uno de sus ejemplos de este error. Lo mismo pasa con *creer*, *opinar*, *considerar*, *decir* o *temer*.

**Truco para comprobarlo** (viene del *Libro de estilo* de la RAE): cambia la parte que empieza por *que* por «eso». Se dice «pienso eso» y no «pienso de eso», así que tampoco va *de* delante de *que*.

**Ojo con el error contrario:** algunos verbos sí piden *de*, sobre todo en su forma con pronombre. Por ejemplo, *me alegro **de** que…*, *me acuerdo **de** que…* o *estoy seguro **de** que…*. Si en esos casos quitas el *de*, cometes el error contrario, el **queísmo**.

Es un error muy común al hablar en muchos países, así que no es raro verlo en el correo de un cliente. Si vas a reutilizar ese texto en algo que se publique, corrígelo.

> *Pienso que deberíamos lanzar la versión en español el lunes.*

- *¿Qué piensas **de** la nueva versión?* ✅
