Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)
No, no es correcto según la norma. Es un caso de **dequeísmo**, es decir, poner un *de* que sobra delante de *que*.

**Forma correcta:**
> Pienso que deberíamos lanzar la versión en español el lunes.

**Por qué:** cuando *pensar* significa "opinar" o "creer", lleva un complemento directo sin preposición. Se dice "pienso algo", no "pienso de algo".

**Truco para comprobarlo:** sustituye la oración que empieza por *que* por *eso*:
- "Pienso **eso**" → suena bien, así que va sin *de*: *pienso que…* ✅
- "Pienso **de eso**" → no tiene sentido, así que *pienso de que* ❌

**Ojo, hay construcciones parecidas que sí son correctas:**
- **Pensar en**, con el sentido de "tener en mente": *Pienso en que el lunes hay mucho tráfico.*
- **Pensar de**, para pedir u opinar sobre algo: *¿Qué piensas de la propuesta?* Aquí *de* va delante de un sustantivo, no de una oración con *que*.

El dequeísmo es muy común en el habla de muchos países, así que no es raro verlo en un correo. Si vas a citar o adaptar ese texto para la versión en español, conviene corregirlo a *pienso que*.

No, no es correcto. Es un caso de **dequeísmo**: poner un "de" antes de "que" cuando el verbo no lo pide.

Lo correcto es:

> **Pienso que** deberíamos lanzar la versión en español el lunes.

**Por qué:** cuando "pensar" significa "opinar" o "creer", va directamente con lo que se piensa. Se piensa *algo*, no *de algo*.

**Truco para comprobarlo:** cambia todo lo que va desde "que" hasta el final por "eso":
- "Pienso **eso**" ✅ → se escribe "pienso que..."
- "Pienso **de eso**" ❌ → no lleva "de"

**Ojo con estos casos, que sí son correctos:**
- "¿Qué piensas **de** la propuesta?" ✅ Aquí "pensar de" significa "tener una opinión sobre algo" y va seguido de un sustantivo, no de una oración con "que".
- "Me acordé **de que** era lunes" ✅ El verbo es "acordarse de algo", así que el "de" sí corresponde. Si lo quitas, el error es el contrario y se llama **queísmo**.

Este error es muy común en el habla de muchos países, así que en un correo de un cliente no vale la pena comentarlo. Pero si vas a citar o reutilizar esa frase en un texto formal o publicado, usa "pienso que".

¿Es correcto decir "pienso de que deberíamos lanzar la versión en español el lunes"? Lo vi en un correo de un cliente.

FAIL if it calls "pienso de que" correct, gives no correction, or claims "the RAE says" without a source and without saying it is unverified.

# Translation: Customer success renewal email (EN → ES)

## Register & audience analysis
This is a B2B renewal and upsell email to a Director of Operations at a Colombian logistics company, on a $180K contract. I used **usted** throughout, as you'd expect for a formal enterprise relationship. "Ustedes" is used where the email means the customer's whole team or company. The source is warm and direct ("Hi Andrés," "Three things on my mind," "level you up"), so I kept it friendly but toned the casual phrases up to match a professional Colombian business register. The opener is shorter, which is normal in Spanish business email. I chose some wording that suits Colombia, since the recipient is known.

## Translation

**Asunto:** Se acerca su renovación: le comparto una breve propuesta

Cordial saludo, Andrés:

Espero que se encuentre muy bien. Quería escribirle personalmente porque la renovación de su contrato con nosotros está prevista para el 15 de junio y, a partir de las conversaciones que nuestro equipo ha tenido con el suyo durante el último trimestre, considero que existe una oportunidad significativa para ampliar nuestra alianza.

Hay tres puntos que quisiera plantearle:

1. En los últimos seis meses, su equipo ha generado un aumento del 40% en el volumen de llamadas a la API, muy por encima de lo que admite su nivel actual. Conviene que pasemos a un nivel superior antes de que lleguen al límite.

2. El nuevo panel de análisis que lanzamos en el primer trimestre no está incluido en su plan, pero su equipo de operaciones le sacaría mucho provecho. Con gusto puedo ofrecerles una prueba de cortesía de 90 días como parte de la renovación.

3. Me gustaría congelar su tarifa actual por licencia durante dos años más, antes de que el aumento de precios entre en vigor en septiembre.

¿Podría dedicarme 20 minutos la próxima semana para revisar juntos estos puntos? Tengo disponibilidad de martes a jueves.

Quedo a la espera de su respuesta.

Cordialmente,

Claire Bennett
Senior Account Executive, Customer Success

## Glossary of key terms

| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| renewal / up for renewal | renovación / está prevista para | Neutral and accurate. "Vence" (expires) would sound more alarming than the source. |
| partnership | alianza | Standard in LatAm B2B. "Sociedad" suggests a legal partnership. |
| API call volume | volumen de llamadas a la API | Standard SaaS term. "API" stays in English. |
| tier | nivel | Kept separate from "plan" because the source uses both words. |
| level you up | pasemos a un nivel superior | A literal version ("subirlos de nivel") sounds like gaming slang. |
| hit the ceiling | lleguen al límite | Natural equivalent. |
| analytics dashboard | panel de análisis | Common in LatAm SaaS. If your product has an official Spanish name for it, use that. |
| Q1 | primer trimestre | Clearer than "T1" in running text. |
| comp you a 90-day trial | ofrecerles una prueba de cortesía de 90 días | "De cortesía" keeps the idea of a complimentary extra, not a generic free trial. |
| per-seat rate | tarifa por licencia | "Por licencia" is the usual LatAm SaaS term. "Por usuario" also works. |
| lock in | congelar | The idiomatic Spanish verb for fixing a price. |

## Translator's notes

1. **No gendered words for either person.** Spanish forces a choice of gender in some words, and I didn't want to assume anyone's gender from their name:
   - **Greeting:** I used "Cordial saludo, Andrés:", a standard formal opener in Colombian business email that doesn't mark gender. If you can confirm how Andrés prefers to be addressed, "Estimado Andrés:" or "Estimada Andrés:" is the more pan-LatAm option.
   - **Closing:** "Quedo a la espera de su respuesta" avoids "quedo atento/atenta."
   - **Signature:** I left the job title in English, which is common in LatAm SaaS signatures. If you want it in Spanish, use "Ejecutiva de Cuentas Sénior, Éxito del Cliente" or the masculine "Ejecutivo," depending on how Claire identifies.
2. **Check the dates before sending.** Today is October 26, 2026. If the renewal is June 15, 2027, the "September" price increase would already be over, or it would mean September 2027, after the renewal. Then point 3's "lock in before the price increase" doesn't hold together. Please confirm the dates. If the timing is still uncertain, consider writing the years in.
3. **"Hi Andrés" raised to a formal greeting.** A bare "Hola, Andrés" combined with usted would sound inconsistent for a $180K enterprise account in Colombia, so I used the more formal opener.
4. **"I think there's a meaningful opportunity"** became "considero que existe una oportunidad significativa." That's slightly more formal than "creo," which suits the register, and the claim is no stronger or weaker than in the source.
5. **"$180K" isn't in the email body**, so there was no currency format to adapt. The "40%" is written without a space, which is the business convention.
✓ pass natural

# Translation: Customer success renewal email (EN → ES)

## Register & audience analysis
This is a business email from an account executive to a Director of Operations at a Colombian logistics company. The relationship is formal and the stakes are high: a USD 180K renewal plus an upsell. I used **usted** throughout, in neutral Latin American Spanish that reads naturally in Colombia, where usted is the expected business register. The English source is warm and a little casual ("level you up," "comp you"). The Spanish keeps the personal, direct tone but uses standard formal B2B wording, so it doesn't sound slangy or pushy to a senior reader.

## Translation

**Asunto:** Próxima renovación de su contrato: una breve propuesta

Estimado Andrés:

Espero que se encuentre muy bien. Quería escribirle personalmente porque su contrato con nosotros vence el 15 de junio y, a partir de las conversaciones que nuestros equipos han sostenido durante el último trimestre, creo que existe una oportunidad importante de ampliar nuestra relación comercial.

Hay tres temas que quisiera plantearle:

1. Su equipo ha generado un aumento del 40 % en el volumen de llamadas a la API durante los últimos seis meses, muy por encima de lo que admite su nivel de servicio actual. Convendría migrarlos a un nivel superior antes de que alcancen el límite.

2. El nuevo panel de analítica que lanzamos en el primer trimestre no está incluido en su plan, pero su equipo de operaciones podría sacarle mucho provecho. Con gusto le ofrecemos una prueba sin costo de 90 días como parte de la renovación.

3. Me gustaría congelar su tarifa actual por usuario durante dos años más, antes de que el aumento de precios entre en vigor en septiembre.

¿Podría dedicarme 20 minutos la próxima semana para revisar esto juntos? Tengo disponibilidad de martes a jueves.

Quedo a la espera de su respuesta.

Cordialmente,

Claire Bennett
Senior Account Executive, Customer Success

## Glossary of key terms

| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| renewal | renovación | Standard term. "Vence" (expires) is used for the contract date because it's more natural than "se renueva". |
| partnership | relación comercial | "Alianza" sounds like a formal strategic alliance, which overstates a vendor–customer relationship. |
| API call volume | volumen de llamadas a la API | "API" stays in English and takes the feminine article (*la API*), as is standard in LatAm tech. |
| tier | nivel de servicio / nivel | Kept separate from "plan" because the source uses two different words. |
| analytics dashboard | panel de analítica | "Dashboard" is also widely used in Colombia, so either works. |
| Q1 | primer trimestre | Spelled out, since "Q1" is less common in Spanish business prose. |
| comp (a trial) | prueba sin costo | "Comp" is US sales slang for "complimentary". |
| per-seat rate | tarifa por usuario | "Por puesto" would be a calque. "Por licencia" is a good alternative. |
| lock in | congelar | Standard way to say a price is held fixed. |

## Translator's notes

1. **"Hi Andrés,"** became "Estimado Andrés:". "Hola Andrés" would read too casual for this register. I kept the first name to preserve the personal relationship the source implies. Spanish formal letters use a colon after the greeting, not a comma.
2. **"level you up"** became "migrarlos a un nivel superior". The gaming-style idiom has no formal Spanish equivalent, and a literal version would sound odd to a director. The meaning is the same.
3. **"Looking forward to it."** became "Quedo a la espera de su respuesta." The more common "Quedo atenta/atento" changes form depending on the sender's gender, which the email doesn't state, so I chose a phrase that doesn't. If Claire prefers "Quedo atenta a su respuesta", that is equally natural.
4. **I left the signature title in English.** Spanish job titles also change with gender (Ejecutiva/Ejecutivo), and many multinationals keep English titles in LatAm correspondence anyway. If you want a Spanish title, a natural option is "Ejecutiva de Cuentas Sénior, Éxito del Cliente".
5. **"40 %"** has a space before the % sign, as the RAE recommends. "40%" without the space is also common in Colombian business writing, so match your house style.
6. **Dates:** the source gives no year for June 15th or September. Since today is October 2026, I assume both refer to 2027. You may want to add the year so the price-increase deadline is unambiguous.
7. **"Three things on my mind"** became "Hay tres temas que quisiera plantearle". A literal "Tengo tres cosas en mente" sounds too casual for a commercial proposal.

If this will be sent as a template to other LATAM customers, I can also run an RAE compliance check with the `rae-spanish` skill.
✓ pass natural

# Translation: Customer success renewal email (EN → ES)

## Register & audience analysis
This is a business email from an account executive to a Director of Operations at a Colombian logistics company. The relationship is formal and the stakes are high: a USD 180K renewal plus an upsell. I used **usted** throughout, in neutral Latin American Spanish that reads naturally in Colombia, where usted is the expected business register. The English source is warm and a little casual ("level you up," "comp you"). The Spanish keeps the personal, direct tone but uses standard formal B2B wording, so it doesn't sound slangy or pushy to a senior reader.

## Translation

**Asunto:** Próxima renovación de su contrato: una breve propuesta

Estimado Andrés:

Espero que se encuentre muy bien. Quería escribirle personalmente porque su contrato con nosotros vence el 15 de junio y, a partir de las conversaciones que nuestros equipos han sostenido durante el último trimestre, creo que existe una oportunidad importante de ampliar nuestra relación comercial.

Hay tres temas que quisiera plantearle:

1. Su equipo ha generado un aumento del 40 % en el volumen de llamadas a la API durante los últimos seis meses, muy por encima de lo que admite su nivel de servicio actual. Convendría migrarlos a un nivel superior antes de que alcancen el límite.

2. El nuevo panel de analítica que lanzamos en el primer trimestre no está incluido en su plan, pero su equipo de operaciones podría sacarle mucho provecho. Con gusto le ofrecemos una prueba sin costo de 90 días como parte de la renovación.

3. Me gustaría congelar su tarifa actual por usuario durante dos años más, antes de que el aumento de precios entre en vigor en septiembre.

¿Podría dedicarme 20 minutos la próxima semana para revisar esto juntos? Tengo disponibilidad de martes a jueves.

Quedo a la espera de su respuesta.

Cordialmente,

Claire Bennett
Senior Account Executive, Customer Success

## Glossary of key terms

| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| renewal | renovación | Standard term. "Vence" (expires) is used for the contract date because it's more natural than "se renueva". |
| partnership | relación comercial | "Alianza" sounds like a formal strategic alliance, which overstates a vendor–customer relationship. |
| API call volume | volumen de llamadas a la API | "API" stays in English and takes the feminine article (*la API*), as is standard in LatAm tech. |
| tier | nivel de servicio / nivel | Kept separate from "plan" because the source uses two different words. |
| analytics dashboard | panel de analítica | "Dashboard" is also widely used in Colombia, so either works. |
| Q1 | primer trimestre | Spelled out, since "Q1" is less common in Spanish business prose. |
| comp (a trial) | prueba sin costo | "Comp" is US sales slang for "complimentary". |
| per-seat rate | tarifa por usuario | "Por puesto" would be a calque. "Por licencia" is a good alternative. |
| lock in | congelar | Standard way to say a price is held fixed. |

## Translator's notes

1. **"Hi Andrés,"** became "Estimado Andrés:". "Hola Andrés" would read too casual for this register. I kept the first name to preserve the personal relationship the source implies. Spanish formal letters use a colon after the greeting, not a comma.
2. **"level you up"** became "migrarlos a un nivel superior". The gaming-style idiom has no formal Spanish equivalent, and a literal version would sound odd to a director. The meaning is the same.
3. **"Looking forward to it."** became "Quedo a la espera de su respuesta." The more common "Quedo atenta/atento" changes form depending on the sender's gender, which the email doesn't state, so I chose a phrase that doesn't. If Claire prefers "Quedo atenta a su respuesta", that is equally natural.
4. **I left the signature title in English.** Spanish job titles also change with gender (Ejecutiva/Ejecutivo), and many multinationals keep English titles in LatAm correspondence anyway. If you want a Spanish title, a natural option is "Ejecutiva de Cuentas Sénior, Éxito del Cliente".
5. **"40 %"** has a space before the % sign, as the RAE recommends. "40%" without the space is also common in Colombian business writing, so match your house style.
6. **Dates:** the source gives no year for June 15th or September. Since today is October 2026, I assume both refer to 2027. You may want to add the year so the price-increase deadline is unambiguous.
7. **"Three things on my mind"** became "Hay tres temas que quisiera plantearle". A literal "Tengo tres cosas en mente" sounds too casual for a commercial proposal.

# Translation: customer success renewal email (EN → ES)

## Register & audience analysis
This is a B2B customer success email to a paying enterprise customer, the Director of Operations at a Colombian logistics company, about a $180K renewal. The English is warm but professional. In Colombian business culture that maps to **usted** throughout, with a cordial tone that isn't stiff. I used neutral Latin American Spanish with vocabulary that sounds natural in Colombia, such as *alianza*, *congelar la tarifa* and *sin costo*. The opener is shorter than the English, as Spanish business emails expect, and the casual phrases ("level you up", "comp you") are rendered professionally without losing their intent.

## Translation

**Asunto:** Se acerca la renovación de su contrato: le comparto una propuesta

Estimado Andrés:

Espero que se encuentre muy bien. Quería escribirle personalmente porque su contrato con nosotros se renueva el 15 de junio y, a partir de las conversaciones que nuestros equipos han sostenido durante el último trimestre, considero que hay una oportunidad significativa para ampliar nuestra alianza.

Quisiera plantearle tres puntos:

1. En los últimos seis meses, su equipo ha generado un aumento del 40 % en el volumen de llamadas a la API, muy por encima de lo que admite su plan actual. Lo ideal sería pasarlos a un plan superior antes de llegar al tope.

2. El nuevo panel de analítica que lanzamos en el primer trimestre no está incluido en su plan, pero su equipo de operaciones le sacaría verdadero provecho. Con gusto podemos ofrecerles una prueba de 90 días sin costo como parte de la renovación.

3. Me gustaría congelar su tarifa actual por usuario durante dos años más, antes de que entre en vigor el aumento de precios en septiembre.

¿Podría dedicarme 20 minutos la próxima semana para revisar estos puntos juntos? Tengo disponibilidad de martes a jueves.

Quedo a la espera de su respuesta.

Cordialmente,

Claire Bennett
Senior Account Executive, Customer Success

## Glossary of key terms

| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| renewal | renovación | Standard term. |
| partnership | alianza | Common in Colombian B2B; it's warmer than *relación comercial*. |
| API call volume | volumen de llamadas a la API | "API" stays as is; it is feminine in Spanish (*la API*). |
| tier / plan | plan | I merged "tier" and "plan" into one term for consistency. |
| level you up | pasarlos a un plan superior | A neutral, professional rendering of a casual idiom. |
| ceiling | tope | Natural in Colombia; *límite* also works. |
| analytics dashboard | panel de analítica | Standard SaaS term in LatAm. |
| comp (complimentary) | sin costo | Preferred over *gratis* in formal B2B writing. |
| lock in [rate] | congelar [la tarifa] | The usual LatAm business idiom for this. |
| per-seat rate | tarifa por usuario | See note 3. |

## Translator's notes

1. **Register.** Andrés is addressed as *usted* throughout. Where the text means his company or team, I used *ustedes* forms ("pasarlos", "ofrecerles"). That shift is natural in Spanish.
2. **Salutation.** "Hi Andrés" is rendered as *Estimado Andrés:*, with the colon Spanish uses after a formal salutation. Using his first name keeps the existing relationship, and *Estimado* sets the right formality for a renewal of this size. If Claire and Andrés are close, *Hola, Andrés:* would also work.
3. **"Per-seat rate"** became *tarifa por usuario*. If your contract or pricing page in Spanish uses *licencia* (e.g., *tarifa por licencia*), change it to match so the email and the contract use the same word.
4. **"Q1"** became *el primer trimestre*. The English doesn't name a year, so neither does the translation. If the email goes out in a different year from the launch, consider adding the year (e.g., *el primer trimestre de 2026*).
5. **Gender agreement.** Spanish makes some words in the sign-off agree with the sender's gender, and I didn't want to guess Claire's.
   - I used *Quedo a la espera de su respuesta*, which doesn't change with gender, instead of *Quedo atento/atenta*.
   - I left the job title in English, which is common in multinational signatures in Colombia. If you want it in Spanish, it's *Ejecutiva Sénior de Cuentas, Éxito del Cliente* or *Ejecutivo Sénior de Cuentas, Éxito del Cliente*. Please confirm which one applies.
6. **"40 %"** has a space before the percent sign, which is the official Spanish (RAE) rule. Many Colombian business documents write *40%* without the space. Either is fine; just use the same style as your other Spanish materials.
7. **"Looking forward to it"** became *Quedo a la espera de su respuesta*. That is the usual formal close in Spanish; a literal translation would sound awkward.

If this email will become a reusable template, I can also check it against the official Spanish grammar and spelling rules (RAE).
✓ pass natural

| comp (complimentary) | sin costo | Preferred over *gratis* in formal B2B writing. |
| lock in [rate] | congelar [la tarifa] | The usual LatAm business idiom for this. |
| per-seat rate | tarifa por usuario | See note 3. |

## Translator's notes

1. **Register.** Andrés is addressed as *usted* throughout. Where the text means his company or team, I used *ustedes* forms ("pasarlos", "ofrecerles"). That shift is natural in Spanish.
2. **Salutation.** "Hi Andrés" is rendered as *Estimado Andrés:*, with the colon Spanish uses after a formal salutation. Using his first name keeps the existing relationship, and *Estimado* sets the right formality for a renewal of this size. If Claire and Andrés are close, *Hola, Andrés:* would also work.
3. **"Per-seat rate"** became *tarifa por usuario*. If your contract or pricing page in Spanish uses *licencia* (e.g., *tarifa por licencia*), change it to match so the email and the contract use the same word.
4. **"Q1"** became *el primer trimestre*. The English doesn't name a year, so neither does the translation. If the email goes out in a different year from the launch, consider adding the year (e.g., *el primer trimestre de 2026*).
5. **Gender agreement.** Spanish makes some words in the sign-off agree with the sender's gender, and I didn't want to guess Claire's.
   - I used *Quedo a la espera de su respuesta*, which doesn't change with gender, instead of *Quedo atento/atenta*.
   - I left the job title in English, which is common in multinational signatures in Colombia. If you want it in Spanish, it's *Ejecutiva Sénior de Cuentas, Éxito del Cliente* or *Ejecutivo Sénior de Cuentas, Éxito del Cliente*. Please confirm which one applies.
6. **"40 %"** has a space before the percent sign, which is the official Spanish (RAE) rule. Many Colombian business documents write *40%* without the space. Either is fine; just use the same style as your other Spanish materials.
7. **"Looking forward to it"** became *Quedo a la espera de su respuesta*. That is the usual formal close in Spanish; a literal translation would sound awkward.

If this email will become a reusable template, I can also check it against the official Spanish grammar and spelling rules (RAE).

# Translation: customer success renewal email (EN → ES)

## Register & audience analysis
This is a B2B customer success email to a paying enterprise customer, the Director of Operations at a Colombian logistics company, about a $180K renewal. The English is warm but professional. In Colombian business culture that maps to **usted** throughout, with a cordial tone that isn't stiff. I used neutral Latin American Spanish with vocabulary that sounds natural in Colombia, such as *alianza*, *congelar la tarifa* and *sin costo*. The opener is shorter than the English, as Spanish business emails expect, and the casual phrases ("level you up", "comp you") are rendered professionally without losing their intent.

## Translation

**Asunto:** Se acerca la renovación de su contrato: le comparto una propuesta

Estimado Andrés:

Espero que se encuentre muy bien. Quería escribirle personalmente porque su contrato con nosotros se renueva el 15 de junio y, a partir de las conversaciones que nuestros equipos han sostenido durante el último trimestre, considero que hay una oportunidad significativa para ampliar nuestra alianza.

Quisiera plantearle tres puntos:

1. En los últimos seis meses, su equipo ha generado un aumento del 40 % en el volumen de llamadas a la API, muy por encima de lo que admite su plan actual. Lo ideal sería pasarlos a un plan superior antes de llegar al tope.

2. El nuevo panel de analítica que lanzamos en el primer trimestre no está incluido en su plan, pero su equipo de operaciones le sacaría verdadero provecho. Con gusto podemos ofrecerles una prueba de 90 días sin costo como parte de la renovación.

3. Me gustaría congelar su tarifa actual por usuario durante dos años más, antes de que entre en vigor el aumento de precios en septiembre.

¿Podría dedicarme 20 minutos la próxima semana para revisar estos puntos juntos? Tengo disponibilidad de martes a jueves.

Quedo a la espera de su respuesta.

Cordialmente,

Claire Bennett
Senior Account Executive, Customer Success

## Glossary of key terms

| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| renewal | renovación | Standard term. |
| partnership | alianza | Common in Colombian B2B; it's warmer than *relación comercial*. |
| API call volume | volumen de llamadas a la API | "API" stays as is; it is feminine in Spanish (*la API*). |
| tier / plan | plan | I merged "tier" and "plan" into one term for consistency. |
| level you up | pasarlos a un plan superior | A neutral, professional rendering of a casual idiom. |
| ceiling | tope | Natural in Colombia; *límite* also works. |
| analytics dashboard | panel de analítica | Standard SaaS term in LatAm. |
| comp (complimentary) | sin costo | Preferred over *gratis* in formal B2B writing. |
| lock in [rate] | congelar [la tarifa] | The usual LatAm business idiom for this. |
| per-seat rate | tarifa por usuario | See note 3. |

## Translator's notes

1. **Register.** Andrés is addressed as *usted* throughout. Where the text means his company or team, I used *ustedes* forms ("pasarlos", "ofrecerles"). That shift is natural in Spanish.
2. **Salutation.** "Hi Andrés" is rendered as *Estimado Andrés:*, with the colon Spanish uses after a formal salutation. Using his first name keeps the existing relationship, and *Estimado* sets the right formality for a renewal of this size. If Claire and Andrés are close, *Hola, Andrés:* would also work.
3. **"Per-seat rate"** became *tarifa por usuario*. If your contract or pricing page in Spanish uses *licencia* (e.g., *tarifa por licencia*), change it to match so the email and the contract use the same word.
4. **"Q1"** became *el primer trimestre*. The English doesn't name a year, so neither does the translation. If the email goes out in a different year from the launch, consider adding the year (e.g., *el primer trimestre de 2026*).
5. **Gender agreement.** Spanish makes some words in the sign-off agree with the sender's gender, and I didn't want to guess Claire's.
   - I used *Quedo a la espera de su respuesta*, which doesn't change with gender, instead of *Quedo atento/atenta*.
   - I left the job title in English, which is common in multinational signatures in Colombia. If you want it in Spanish, it's *Ejecutiva Sénior de Cuentas, Éxito del Cliente* or *Ejecutivo Sénior de Cuentas, Éxito del Cliente*. Please confirm which one applies.
6. **"40 %"** has a space before the percent sign, which is the official Spanish (RAE) rule. Many Colombian business documents write *40%* without the space. Either is fine; just use the same style as your other Spanish materials.
7. **"Looking forward to it"** became *Quedo a la espera de su respuesta*. That is the usual formal close in Spanish; a literal translation would sound awkward.

If this email will become a reusable template, I can also check it against the official Spanish grammar and spelling rules (RAE).

**Asunto:** Próxima renovación de su contrato: una propuesta para su consideración

Estimado Andrés:

Espero que se encuentre muy bien. Quería escribirle personalmente porque su contrato con nosotros se renueva el próximo 15 de junio. Por lo que nuestros equipos han conversado durante el último trimestre, creo que tenemos una buena oportunidad de ampliar nuestra alianza.

Quisiera compartirle tres puntos:

1. En los últimos seis meses, su equipo ha aumentado en un 40 % el volumen de llamadas a la API, muy por encima de lo que cubre su plan actual. Le recomendaría pasar a un plan superior antes de llegar al límite.

2. El nuevo panel de analítica que lanzamos en el primer trimestre no está incluido en su plan actual, pero creo que sería muy útil para su equipo de operaciones. Con gusto podemos ofrecerle una prueba sin costo de 90 días como parte de la renovación.

3. Me gustaría mantener su tarifa actual por usuario durante dos años más, antes de que el aumento de precios entre en vigor en septiembre.

¿Tendría disponibles 20 minutos la próxima semana para revisar esta propuesta juntos? Tengo disponibilidad de martes a jueves.

Quedo atenta a su respuesta.

Cordialmente,

**Claire Bennett**
Ejecutiva Sénior de Cuentas, Éxito del Cliente

---

**Translation choices:**
- **"Usted" throughout, with "Estimado Andrés" and "Cordialmente":** This is the norm in Colombian business writing, especially with a director-level client on a large contract. "Hi" became a formal greeting.
- **Casual phrases made more formal:** "Level you up" became "pasar a un plan superior" (move to a higher plan). "Comp you" became "ofrecerle una prueba sin costo" (offer you a free trial). Literal versions would sound too casual or unclear in Spanish.
- **"Quedo atenta a su respuesta":** This is a common Latin American closing. I used the feminine "atenta" because it refers to Claire herself, so it assumes she uses she/her. If not, "Quedo pendiente de su respuesta" is gender-neutral. Change it before sending if needed.
- **"Tarifa por usuario":** I used this for "per-seat rate." If your Spanish contracts say "licencia" or "puesto," use that word instead so it matches.

**Check the dates:** Today is October 26, 2026, so June 15 and the September price increase have both already passed. If this is going out for a 2027 renewal, update both dates, and consider adding the year to avoid confusion.

**Translation choices:**
- **Formal but warm:** I used *usted* throughout, which fits the relationship. "Apreciado" and "Cordialmente" are standard in Colombian business email and sound warmer than "Estimado" or "Atentamente."
- **Casual phrases:** "Level you up" and "hit the ceiling" became "pasar a un nivel superior" and "llegar al límite." The literal versions sound too casual in Spanish. "Comp you a trial" became "prueba de cortesía."
- **Gender:** Spanish often marks the writer's gender, as in *atenta/atento* or *segura/seguro*. I worded around those so the email works no matter who sends it, and kept the job title in English for the same reason. Many LATAM companies keep English titles anyway. If you want it in Spanish: *Ejecutiva/Ejecutivo Sénior de Cuentas, Éxito del Cliente*.
- **"Per-seat":** I used "por usuario." If your contracts say "licencia," use "por licencia" so the email matches.

**Asunto:** Se acerca la renovación de su contrato: una propuesta para su consideración

Apreciado Andrés:

Espero que se encuentre muy bien. Le escribo personalmente porque su contrato con nosotros se renueva el próximo 15 de junio. Por las conversaciones que nuestros equipos han tenido durante el último trimestre, creo que hay una oportunidad importante de ampliar nuestra alianza.

Quisiera proponerle tres puntos:

1. En los últimos seis meses, su equipo ha aumentado en un 40 % el volumen de llamadas a la API, muy por encima de lo que cubre su plan actual. Le recomendamos pasar a un plan superior antes de que lleguen al límite.

2. El nuevo panel de analítica que lanzamos en el primer trimestre no está incluido en su plan actual, pero sería de gran valor para su equipo de operaciones. Con gusto le ofrecemos una prueba sin costo de 90 días como parte de la renovación.

3. Me gustaría mantenerle la tarifa actual por usuario durante dos años más, antes de que el aumento de precios entre en vigor en septiembre.

¿Tendría disponibles 20 minutos la próxima semana para revisar juntos esta propuesta? Tengo disponibilidad de martes a jueves.

Quedo atenta a su respuesta.

Cordialmente,

Claire Bennett
Ejecutiva Sénior de Cuentas, Éxito del Cliente

---

**Translation choices:**

- **Formal "usted" throughout.** "Apreciado" and "Cordialmente" are the usual greeting and sign-off in Colombian business email. They are warmer than "Estimado/Saludos" but still professional.
- **Idioms were rewritten, not translated word for word.** "Level you up before you hit the ceiling" became a recommendation to move to a higher plan. "Comp you" became "ofrecerle... sin costo" (offer it at no cost). A literal version would sound too casual for a $180K account.
- **"Quedo atenta a su respuesta"** ("I look forward to your reply") replaces "Looking forward to it," which sounds abrupt in Spanish. The feminine "atenta" assumes Claire uses feminine forms. If not, use "Quedo pendiente de su respuesta," which has no gender.
- **"Éxito del Cliente"** is the common LATAM title for Customer Success. Some companies keep "Customer Success" in English, so use whatever your team uses.

**Check the dates before sending:** today is October 26, 2026, so "June 15" and "September" have already passed this year. If the email is for an upcoming renewal, update both dates or add the year (e.g., "el 15 de junio de 2027") so it's clear.

Prompt

Translate this customer success email to Spanish for our LATAM enterprise customers. The recipient is a Director of Operations at a logistics company in Colombia. We have a formal relationship — they're a paying customer renewing a $180K annual contract.

Subject: Your renewal is coming up — quick proposal inside

Hi Andrés,

I hope this email finds you well. I wanted to reach out personally because your contract with us is up for renewal on June 15th, and based on the conversations our team has had with yours over the past quarter, I think there's a meaningful opportunity to expand the partnership.

Three things on my mind:

  1. Your team has driven a 40% increase in API call volume over the last six months — well beyond what your current tier supports. We should level you up before you hit the ceiling.

  2. The new analytics dashboard we shipped in Q1 isn't available on your plan, but your operations team would get real value from it. Happy to comp you a 90-day trial as part of the renewal.

  3. I'd like to lock in your current per-seat rate for another two years before the price increase takes effect in September.

Could you spare 20 minutes next week to walk through this together? I'm flexible Tuesday through Thursday.

Looking forward to it.

Best, Claire Bennett Senior Account Executive, Customer Success

Graders — what "good" means for this case
faithfulllm

PASS if every fact survives unchanged: renewal on June 15, 40% growth over six months, the Q1 analytics dashboard not on their plan, a 90-day trial, a two-year rate lock before a September price increase, a 20-minute call Tuesday to Thursday, the signer's name and title. FAIL if any fact is missing, changed, softened or added.

naturalllm

PASS if the opening, the idioms ("level you up", "hit the ceiling", "comp you a 90-day trial", "lock in your rate") and the sign-off read as natural Latin American business Spanish rather than literal calques, and the closing is formal (for example Atentamente, Cordialmente, Saludos cordiales). FAIL if any idiom is rendered word for word, or the closing is casual.

registerllm

PASS if the whole email uses usted consistently, which suits a formal B2B relationship with a director. FAIL if any tú form appears.

reportllm

PASS if the reply includes a short register analysis and translator's notes that explain at least two specific choices (for example the register, a transcreated idiom, how the date or title was handled). FAIL if there are no notes.

# Translation: API integration platform UI strings (EN → ES, neutral Latin American Spanish)

## Register & audience analysis
These are UI strings for a B2B developer platform, read by engineering managers and senior developers. To match the clinical, formal voice you described, I used *usted*. Where possible I used impersonal *se* constructions ("Se eliminarán…", "Se alcanzó…") so the text doesn't address the reader directly. Buttons use bare infinitives, which is the standard for formal Spanish UI. I kept English the technical terms that Spanish-speaking developers normally leave in English (*endpoint*, *webhook*, *API*, *stack*).

## Translation
```json
{
  "button.save": "Guardar cambios",
  "button.delete": "Eliminar",
  "button.cancel": "Cancelar",
  "button.invite": "Invitar a un miembro",
  "confirm.delete.title": "¿Eliminar {{count}} integraciones?",
  "confirm.delete.body": "Se eliminarán de forma permanente {{count}} integraciones de su espacio de trabajo. Esta acción no se puede deshacer.",
  "error.rate_limit": "Se alcanzó el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {{seconds}} segundos.",
  "empty_state.title": "Aún no hay integraciones",
  "empty_state.body": "Conecte su primera API para comenzar a sincronizar datos en todo su stack tecnológico.",
  "toast.invite_sent": "Invitación enviada a {{email}}",
  "webhook.help": "El endpoint de su webhook debe devolver un código 2xx en 5 segundos o menos.",
  "settings.api_key.label": "Clave de API",
  "settings.api_key.help": "Use esta clave en el encabezado Authorization de sus solicitudes."
}
```

## Glossary of key terms
| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| integration | integración | Same word in every string |
| workspace | espacio de trabajo | Use this everywhere in the product |
| delete / remove | eliminar | One verb for both. *Remover* is a false friend (it means "to stir") |
| rate limit | límite de solicitudes | More precise for an API than *límite de uso*. See note 4 |
| request | solicitud | Standard in UI text |
| API key | clave de API | Standard term across Latin America |
| header | encabezado | See note 6 |
| endpoint, webhook, API, stack | kept in English | These are the established terms in Spanish developer writing |
| teammate | miembro | See note 1 |

## Translator's notes
1. **"Invite teammate"**: The literal "Invitar a un compañero de equipo" is 32 characters, over your 25-character limit. I used "Invitar a un miembro" (20). *Miembro* is the same for any gender and reads clearly when you're in a workspace. Another option is "Invitar a colaborador" (21), but *colaborador* is grammatically masculine.
2. **`{{count}}` plurals**: Both confirmation strings assume more than one integration. If `count` can be 1, "¿Eliminar 1 integraciones?" will be wrong, just as "Delete 1 integrations?" is wrong in English. I recommend ICU plural syntax, for example `{count, plural, one {¿Eliminar # integración?} other {¿Eliminar # integraciones?}}`. The body string would need the same treatment.
3. **Formal address**: Imperatives use *usted* ("Conecte", "Use", "Vuelva a intentarlo"). Two strings use impersonal constructions to keep the tone clinical rather than personal ("You've hit the rate limit" became "Se alcanzó el límite…").
4. **"rate limit"**: Some teams keep "rate limit" in English in their docs. If your Spanish documentation already does that, change this string to match.
5. **"webhook.help"**: My first version was 1.4× the English length, so I shortened it. "Within 5 seconds" became "en 5 segundos o menos", which keeps the meaning of "up to 5 seconds". I also shortened "código de estado 2xx" to "código 2xx", which senior developers will read without trouble. If you have room, "código de estado 2xx" is slightly more precise.
6. **"Authorization header"**: The header name stays exactly as written because it's a literal identifier. For "header" itself I used *encabezado*, which Spanish-language references such as MDN use. If your docs say "header", use that here too. Consider showing `Authorization` in code formatting if the UI supports it.
7. **"across your stack"**: I wrote "en todo su stack tecnológico". If you'd rather avoid the anglicism, "en todos sus sistemas" also works.

**QA:** I ran the `l10n-qa` checker on the final file, with a 25-character limit on buttons and 40 on confirmation titles. It found 0 errors and 0 warnings. All placeholders are intact, every key is present, and all buttons are within the limit (longest: "Invitar a un miembro", 20 characters).
✓ pass limits-and-plurals

judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

Judge votes✓ ✓ ✓
Evidence (what the judge was shown; for an image, a description of what was sent)

```json
{
  "button.save": "Guardar cambios",
  "button.delete": "Eliminar",
  "button.cancel": "Cancelar",
  "button.invite": "Invitar a un miembro",
  "confirm.delete.title": "¿Eliminar {{count}} integraciones?",
  "confirm.delete.body": "Se eliminarán de forma permanente {{count}} integraciones de su espacio de trabajo. Esta acción no se puede deshacer.",
  "error.rate_limit": "Se alcanzó el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {{seconds}} segundos.",
  "empty_state.title": "Aún no hay integraciones",
  "empty_state.body": "Conecte su primera API para comenzar a sincronizar datos en todo su stack tecnológico.",
  "toast.invite_sent": "Invitación enviada a {{email}}",
  "webhook.help": "El endpoint de su webhook debe devolver un código 2xx en 5 segundos o menos.",
  "settings.api_key.label": "Clave de API",
  "settings.api_key.help": "Use esta clave en el encabezado Authorization de sus solicitudes."
}
```

## Glossary of key terms
| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| integration | integración | Same word in every string |
| workspace | espacio de trabajo | Use this everywhere in the product |
| delete / remove | eliminar | One verb for both. *Remover* is a false friend (it means "to stir") |
| rate limit | límite de solicitudes | More precise for an API than *límite de uso*. See note 4 |
| request | solicitud | Standard in UI text |
| API key | clave de API | Standard term across Latin America |
| header | encabezado | See note 6 |
| endpoint, webhook, API, stack | kept in English | These are the established terms in Spanish developer writing |
| teammate | miembro | See note 1 |

## Translator's notes
1. **"Invite teammate"**: The literal "Invitar a un compañero de equipo" is 32 characters, over your 25-character limit. I used "Invitar a un miembro" (20). *Miembro* is the same for any gender and reads clearly when you're in a workspace. Another option is "Invitar a colaborador" (21), but *colaborador* is grammatically masculine.
2. **`{{count}}` plurals**: Both confirmation strings assume more than one integration. If `count` can be 1, "¿Eliminar 1 integraciones?" will be wrong, just as "Delete 1 integrations?" is wrong in English. I recommend ICU plural syntax, for example `{count, plural, one {¿Eliminar # integración?} other {¿Eliminar # integraciones?}}`. The body string would need the same treatment.
3. **Formal address**: Imperatives use *usted* ("Conecte", "Use", "Vuelva a intentarlo"). Two strings use impersonal constructions to keep the tone clinical rather than personal ("You've hit the rate limit" became "Se alcanzó el límite…").
4. **"rate limit"**: Some teams keep "rate limit" in English in their docs. If your Spanish documentation already does that, change this string to match.
5. **"webhook.help"**: My first version was 1.4× the English length, so I shortened it. "Within 5 seconds" became "en 5 segundos o menos", which keeps the meaning of "up to 5 seconds". I also shortened "código de estado 2xx" to "código 2xx", which senior developers will read without trouble. If you have room, "código de estado 2xx" is slightly more precise.
6. **"Authorization header"**: The header name stays exactly as written because it's a literal identifier. For "header" itself I used *encabezado*, which Spanish-language references such as MDN use. If your docs say "header", use that here too. Consider showing `Authorization` in code formatting if the UI supports it.
7. **"across your stack"**: I wrote "en todo su stack tecnológico". If you'd rather avoid the anglicism, "en todos sus sistemas" also works.

**QA:** I ran the `l10n-qa` checker on the final file, with a 25-character limit on buttons and 40 on confirmation titles. It found 0 errors and 0 warnings. All placeholders are intact, every key is present, and all buttons are within the limit (longest: "Invitar a un miembro", 20 characters).

# Translation: UI strings for an API-integration developer platform (EN → ES, neutral LatAm)

## Register & audience analysis
These are UI strings for a B2B developer product, read by engineering managers and senior developers. I used formal *usted* throughout (*Conecte*, *Vuelva a intentarlo*, *su espacio de trabajo*). Where the English allows it, I used impersonal constructions (*Se eliminarán…*, *Se alcanzó…*), which give the clinical, Stripe-like voice you asked for. There's no *por favor*, no exclamation marks and no warm filler. Standard developer terms (*endpoint*, *webhook*, *stack*, `Authorization`, `2xx`) stay in English, as Spanish-speaking developers expect.

```json
  "button.invite": "Invitar a un miembro",

  "confirm.delete.body": "Se eliminarán de forma permanente {{count}} integraciones de su espacio de trabajo. Esta acción no se puede deshacer.",
  "error.rate_limit": "Se alcanzó el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {{seconds}} segundos.",
  "empty_state.title": "Aún no hay integraciones",

  "toast.invite_sent": "Invitación enviada a {{email}}",
  "webhook.help": "El endpoint de su webhook debe devolver un código de estado 2xx en 5 segundos o menos.",
  "settings.api_key.label": "Clave de API",
  "settings.api_key.help": "Use esta clave en el encabezado Authorization de sus solicitudes."
}
```

## Glossary of key terms

| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Delete / remove | Eliminar | Used for both the button and the body text so the action is named consistently. Avoids the false friend *remover* ("to stir"). |
| Integration | Integración | |
| Workspace | Espacio de trabajo | Use it the same way everywhere else in the product. |
| Teammate | Miembro | *Miembro* is the same word for any gender, which avoids *compañero/a*. |
| Rate limit | Límite de solicitudes | See note 3. |
| Request | Solicitud | Matches *límite de solicitudes*. |
| Header | Encabezado | See note 5. |
| API key | Clave de API | The standard LatAm term (Google and AWS use it). |
| Endpoint, webhook, stack | Unchanged | These are the settled terms in Spanish developer writing. |
| Status code | Código de estado | |

## Translator's notes

1. **Length check.** I ran the l10n-qa checker with a 25-character limit on buttons. It finds no errors or warnings, and every placeholder (`{{count}}` ×2, `{{seconds}}`, `{{email}}`) is unchanged. The longest button is *Invitar a un miembro* at 20 characters.
2. **`confirm.delete.title` is 34 characters**, against 30 for the English. With a 25-character cap on titles it fails, but the English source already goes over 25, so I treated "roughly the same as English" as the target. I didn't switch to *Borrar*, which would save 2 characters but break consistency with the *Eliminar* button. If titles have a hard 25-character limit, I'd suggest a shorter fallback: *¿Eliminar integraciones?* (24), with the count moved to the body text.
3. **"rate limit"** became *límite de solicitudes*, which is clear to managers and developers alike. Many LatAm developers also say *rate limit*. Switch to that if your API docs keep the English term.
4. **Plurals.** `{{count}}` and `{{seconds}}` are plural-only, both in the source and in Spanish. With 1 they would show "¿Eliminar 1 integraciones?" and "1 segundos". I'd recommend ICU plural forms for these keys, e.g. `{count, plural, one {¿Eliminar # integración?} other {¿Eliminar # integraciones?}}`.
5. **"Authorization header"** became *encabezado Authorization*. MDN in Spanish and Stripe's Spanish docs both use *encabezado*. The header name itself stays in English because it's a literal token. Some teams prefer *header Authorization*, so make it match your docs.
6. **"within 5 seconds"** became *en 5 segundos o menos*. This keeps the inclusive meaning (≤ 5 s) and is shorter than *en un plazo máximo de*. I shortened it after the checker warned that my first version was 1.4× the English length.
7. **"across your stack"** became *en todo su stack*. If you want a fully Spanish wording for less technical readers, *en todos sus sistemas* works.
8. **"Invite sent"** became *Invitación enviada*. Spanish has no direct equivalent of the clipped noun "invite", so the full noun is the natural form.

# Translation: Developer-platform UI strings (EN → ES, neutral LatAm)

## Register & audience analysis
This is technical SaaS UI copy for engineering managers and senior developers. It addresses the user as **usted** throughout. Where English puts the user at the center of an error, the Spanish uses the **impersonal se** instead (e.g., "Se alcanzó el límite…" rather than "Usted alcanzó…"), which gives the neutral, blameless tone you get from Stripe. There's no *por favor*, no exclamation marks and no filler. Developer terms that LatAm engineers normally leave in English (*endpoint*, *webhook*, *API*, *stack*, the `Authorization` header name) stay in English. Everything else is translated.

## Translation

```json
{
  "button.save": "Guardar cambios",
  "button.delete": "Eliminar",
  "button.cancel": "Cancelar",
  "button.invite": "Invitar a un colega",
  "confirm.delete.title": "¿Eliminar {{count}} integraciones?",
  "confirm.delete.body": "Se eliminarán de forma permanente {{count}} integraciones de su espacio de trabajo. Esta acción no se puede deshacer.",
  "error.rate_limit": "Se alcanzó el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {{seconds}} segundos.",
  "empty_state.title": "Aún no hay integraciones",
  "empty_state.body": "Conecte su primera API para comenzar a sincronizar datos en todo su stack tecnológico.",
  "toast.invite_sent": "Invitación enviada a {{email}}",
  "webhook.help": "El endpoint de su webhook debe devolver un código de estado 2xx en un máximo de 5 segundos.",
  "settings.api_key.label": "Clave de API",
  "settings.api_key.help": "Utilice esta clave en el encabezado Authorization de sus solicitudes."
}
```

**Button lengths (limit 25):** Guardar cambios (15), Eliminar (8), Cancelar (8), Invitar a un colega (19).

## Glossary of key terms

| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| integration | integración | Standard term. |
| workspace | espacio de trabajo | Use this everywhere, never just *espacio* or *área de trabajo*. |
| delete / remove | eliminar | Not *remover*, which means "to stir." *Eliminar* is used for both the button and the body text so they match. |
| rate limit | límite de solicitudes | Clearer than *límite de uso*. It also matches *solicitudes* for "requests" (see below). |
| request | solicitud | Matches *límite de solicitudes*. |
| teammate | colega | See note 3. |
| API key | clave de API | Standard term (Stripe and Google use it). Not *llave*. |
| header | encabezado | See note 5. |
| endpoint, webhook, API, stack | *(unchanged)* | These are the terms LatAm developers actually use. |
| status code | código de estado | Standard term. |
| invite (noun) | invitación | |

## Translator's notes

1. **Placeholders and plurals.** "{{count}} integrations" and "{{seconds}} seconds" only work when the number is 2 or more. When `count` = 1 you'd get "¿Eliminar 1 integraciones?", which is just as wrong in Spanish as it is in English. I recommend ICU plurals in both languages, for example `{count, plural, one {¿Eliminar # integración?} other {¿Eliminar # integraciones?}}`. If your i18n library handles plurals by key (i18next uses `_one` / `_other`), I can send the split keys.
2. **"You've hit the rate limit"** is rendered impersonally as *Se alcanzó el límite de solicitudes*. "You've hit" in Spanish would sound blaming and informal for this voice. *Vuelva a intentarlo* keeps the instruction in usted.
3. **"Invite teammate"** → *Invitar a un colega* (19 characters). The literal *Invitar a un miembro del equipo* is 31, which is over the limit. If your product calls workspace users "members" elsewhere, use *Invitar a un miembro* (20) so the terms match.
4. **"Delete {{count}} integrations?"** In Spanish the opening ¿ is required. The title is 34 characters against 30 in English, about 13% longer. There's no shorter natural wording, so check it in the confirmation modal.
5. **"Authorization header"** → *encabezado Authorization*. The header name stays exactly as written. Stripe's and MDN's Spanish docs use *encabezado*. If your docs keep *header* in English, change it here as well.
6. **"across your stack"** → *en todo su stack tecnológico*. *Stack* is common LatAm dev usage, and *tecnológico* stops it reading as a bare anglicism. *en toda su infraestructura* is a more formal option, but it's a bit broader in meaning.
7. **"within 5 seconds"** → *en un máximo de 5 segundos*. I first wrote *en un plazo máximo de…*, but the QA check flagged it as 1.4× the English length, so I shortened it. The meaning is the same: 5 seconds is allowed. *en menos de* would subtly exclude exactly 5 seconds.
8. **No period on the toast and titles**, matching the English. Body and help text keep their periods.

**QA:** I ran the `l10n-qa` checker with a 25-character limit on `button.*` and a list of terms to keep in English. On the final file it found 0 errors, 0 warnings and 0 notes. All placeholders (`{{count}}`, `{{seconds}}`, `{{email}}`) are unchanged, and register and terminology are consistent across all 13 strings.
✓ pass limits-and-plurals

judge votes: PASS PASS PASS

# Translation: Developer-platform UI strings (EN → ES, neutral LatAm)

## Register & audience analysis
This is technical SaaS UI copy for engineering managers and senior developers. It addresses the user as **usted** throughout. Where English puts the user at the center of an error, the Spanish uses the **impersonal se** instead (e.g., "Se alcanzó el límite…" rather than "Usted alcanzó…"), which gives the neutral, blameless tone you get from Stripe. There's no *por favor*, no exclamation marks and no filler. Developer terms that LatAm engineers normally leave in English (*endpoint*, *webhook*, *API*, *stack*, the `Authorization` header name) stay in English. Everything else is translated.

## Translation

```json
{
  "button.save": "Guardar cambios",
  "button.delete": "Eliminar",
  "button.cancel": "Cancelar",
  "button.invite": "Invitar a un colega",
  "confirm.delete.title": "¿Eliminar {{count}} integraciones?",
  "confirm.delete.body": "Se eliminarán de forma permanente {{count}} integraciones de su espacio de trabajo. Esta acción no se puede deshacer.",
  "error.rate_limit": "Se alcanzó el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {{seconds}} segundos.",
  "empty_state.title": "Aún no hay integraciones",
  "empty_state.body": "Conecte su primera API para comenzar a sincronizar datos en todo su stack tecnológico.",
  "toast.invite_sent": "Invitación enviada a {{email}}",
  "webhook.help": "El endpoint de su webhook debe devolver un código de estado 2xx en un máximo de 5 segundos.",
  "settings.api_key.label": "Clave de API",
  "settings.api_key.help": "Utilice esta clave en el encabezado Authorization de sus solicitudes."
}
```

**Button lengths (limit 25):** Guardar cambios (15), Eliminar (8), Cancelar (8), Invitar a un colega (19).

## Glossary of key terms

| Source | Target | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| integration | integración | Standard term. |
| workspace | espacio de trabajo | Use this everywhere, never just *espacio* or *área de trabajo*. |
| delete / remove | eliminar | Not *remover*, which means "to stir." *Eliminar* is used for both the button and the body text so they match. |
| rate limit | límite de solicitudes | Clearer than *límite de uso*. It also matches *solicitudes* for "requests" (see below). |
| request | solicitud | Matches *límite de solicitudes*. |
| teammate | colega | See note 3. |
| API key | clave de API | Standard term (Stripe and Google use it). Not *llave*. |
| header | encabezado | See note 5. |
| endpoint, webhook, API, stack | *(unchanged)* | These are the terms LatAm developers actually use. |
| status code | código de estado | Standard term. |
| invite (noun) | invitación | |

## Translator's notes

1. **Placeholders and plurals.** "{{count}} integrations" and "{{seconds}} seconds" only work when the number is 2 or more. When `count` = 1 you'd get "¿Eliminar 1 integraciones?", which is just as wrong in Spanish as it is in English. I recommend ICU plurals in both languages, for example `{count, plural, one {¿Eliminar # integración?} other {¿Eliminar # integraciones?}}`. If your i18n library handles plurals by key (i18next uses `_one` / `_other`), I can send the split keys.
2. **"You've hit the rate limit"** is rendered impersonally as *Se alcanzó el límite de solicitudes*. "You've hit" in Spanish would sound blaming and informal for this voice. *Vuelva a intentarlo* keeps the instruction in usted.
3. **"Invite teammate"** → *Invitar a un colega* (19 characters). The literal *Invitar a un miembro del equipo* is 31, which is over the limit. If your product calls workspace users "members" elsewhere, use *Invitar a un miembro* (20) so the terms match.
4. **"Delete {{count}} integrations?"** In Spanish the opening ¿ is required. The title is 34 characters against 30 in English, about 13% longer. There's no shorter natural wording, so check it in the confirmation modal.
5. **"Authorization header"** → *encabezado Authorization*. The header name stays exactly as written. Stripe's and MDN's Spanish docs use *encabezado*. If your docs keep *header* in English, change it here as well.
6. **"across your stack"** → *en todo su stack tecnológico*. *Stack* is common LatAm dev usage, and *tecnológico* stops it reading as a bare anglicism. *en toda su infraestructura* is a more formal option, but it's a bit broader in meaning.
7. **"within 5 seconds"** → *en un máximo de 5 segundos*. I first wrote *en un plazo máximo de…*, but the QA check flagged it as 1.4× the English length, so I shortened it. The meaning is the same: 5 seconds is allowed. *en menos de* would subtly exclude exactly 5 seconds.
8. **No period on the toast and titles**, matching the English. Body and help text keep their periods.

**QA:** I ran the `l10n-qa` checker with a 25-character limit on `button.*` and a list of terms to keep in English. On the final file it found 0 errors, 0 warnings and 0 notes. All placeholders (`{{count}}`, `{{seconds}}`, `{{email}}`) are unchanged, and register and terminology are consistent across all 13 strings.

matched \{\{count\}\}[\s\S]*\{\{seconds\}\}[\s\S]*\{\{email\}\}

✓ pass register

```json
{
  "button.save": "Guardar cambios",
  "button.delete": "Eliminar",
  "button.cancel": "Cancelar",
  "button.invite": "Invitar a un miembro",
  "confirm.delete.title": "¿Eliminar {{count}} integraciones?",
  "confirm.delete.body": "Se eliminarán permanentemente {{count}} integraciones de su espacio de trabajo. Esta acción no se puede deshacer.",
  "error.rate_limit": "Se alcanzó el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {{seconds}} segundos.",
  "empty_state.title": "Aún no hay integraciones",
  "empty_state.body": "Conecte su primera API para comenzar a sincronizar datos en todo su stack.",
  "toast.invite_sent": "Invitación enviada a {{email}}",
  "webhook.help": "Su endpoint de webhook debe devolver un código de estado 2xx en un máximo de 5 segundos.",
  "settings.api_key.label": "Clave de API",
  "settings.api_key.help": "Utilice esta clave en el encabezado Authorization de sus solicitudes."
}
```

## Translator's report

**Locale and voice**
- **Neutral Latin American Spanish (es-419).** I avoided regional terms: no *vosotros*, no voseo, and no Spain-specific words like *ordenador* or *vale*.
- **Formal "usted" everywhere.** This fits the clinical, formal brand voice. Commands use the usted form (*Conecte*, *Utilice*, *Vuelva a intentarlo*). Where it read naturally, I used impersonal phrasing instead (*Se eliminarán…*, *Se alcanzó…*), which sounds more neutral, like Stripe.
- If the brand later moves to "tú", these four strings need changing: `confirm.delete.body`, `error.rate_limit`, `empty_state.body`, `webhook.help` and `settings.api_key.help`. For example, *su* becomes *tu* and *Conecte* becomes *Conecta*.

**Placeholders**
- All 4 are kept exactly as written: `{{count}}` (×2), `{{seconds}}`, `{{email}}`. None were translated or moved somewhere that breaks the grammar.

**Length (button limit is 25 characters)**
| Key | EN | ES |
|---|---|---|
| button.save | 12 | 15 |
| button.delete | 6 | 8 |
| button.cancel | 6 | 8 |
| button.invite | 15 | 20 |
| confirm.delete.title | 30 | 34 (with the placeholder) |

- All buttons are under 25 characters.
- The confirmation title is about 13% longer than the English, which is normal for Spanish. "Integraciones" can't be shortened without losing clarity.
- For `button.invite`, I chose *miembro* for "teammate" because it matches the workspace/member model. *Invitar a un miembro del equipo* (31) was too long.

**Terminology choices**
- **rate limit → *límite de solicitudes*.** The literal *límite de tasa* is a calque (word-for-word copy) that sounds odd to LatAm developers. *Límite de solicitudes* says the same thing clearly.
- **Kept in English:** *endpoint*, *webhook*, *stack*, *API* and the header name *Authorization*. LatAm developers use these words as-is. Translated versions (*punto de conexión*, *pila tecnológica*) sound like machine translation to this audience. *Authorization* has to stay literal because it's the actual HTTP header name.
- **API key → *Clave de API*.** This is the standard term (Stripe, Google Cloud and AWS es-419 all use it).
- **workspace → *espacio de trabajo*.** If the product UI uses "Workspace" as a branded feature name, keep it in English for consistency.
- **within 5 seconds → *en un máximo de 5 segundos*.** This keeps the deadline meaning clearer than *dentro de 5 segundos*.
- **API is feminine** (*su primera API*), which is the standard usage.

**Issues for engineering (they affect the English too)**
1. **Plurals:** `confirm.delete.title`, `confirm.delete.body` and `error.rate_limit` are written only in the plural. With `count = 1` you'd get *"¿Eliminar 1 integraciones?"*. I recommend splitting them into plural keys: `_one`/`_other` if you use i18next, or ICU `{count, plural, one {…} other {…}}`. For example:
   - `confirm.delete.title_one`: `"¿Eliminar {{count}} integración?"`
   - `confirm.delete.title_other`: `"¿Eliminar {{count}} integraciones?"`
2. **Number formatting:** if `{{count}}` or `{{seconds}}` can go above 999, format them by locale. es-419 usually writes `1,000` (Mexico) but some countries write `1.000`. The neutral choice is to use `Intl.NumberFormat('es-419')`.

**Items to check in context**
- `toast.invite_sent`: *Invitación enviada* is masculine/feminine-safe and doesn't depend on who was invited.
- `empty_state.title` is 24 characters. Check it in the empty-state layout if that container has a fixed width.
✓ pass limits-and-plurals

```json
  "button.delete": "Eliminar",
  "button.cancel": "Cancelar",
  "button.invite": "Invitar a un miembro",
  "confirm.delete.title": "¿Eliminar {{count}} integraciones?",
  "confirm.delete.body": "Se eliminarán permanentemente {{count}} integraciones de su espacio de trabajo. Esta acción no se puede deshacer.",
  "error.rate_limit": "Se alcanzó el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {{seconds}} segundos.",
  "empty_state.title": "Aún no hay integraciones",
  "empty_state.body": "Conecte su primera API para comenzar a sincronizar datos en todo su stack.",
  "toast.invite_sent": "Invitación enviada a {{email}}",
  "webhook.help": "Su endpoint de webhook debe devolver un código de estado 2xx en un máximo de 5 segundos.",
  "settings.api_key.label": "Clave de API",
  "settings.api_key.help": "Utilice esta clave en el encabezado Authorization de sus solicitudes."
}
```

## Translator's report

**Locale and voice**
- **Neutral Latin American Spanish (es-419).** I avoided regional terms: no *vosotros*, no voseo, and no Spain-specific words like *ordenador* or *vale*.
- **Formal "usted" everywhere.** This fits the clinical, formal brand voice. Commands use the usted form (*Conecte*, *Utilice*, *Vuelva a intentarlo*). Where it read naturally, I used impersonal phrasing instead (*Se eliminarán…*, *Se alcanzó…*), which sounds more neutral, like Stripe.
- If the brand later moves to "tú", these four strings need changing: `confirm.delete.body`, `error.rate_limit`, `empty_state.body`, `webhook.help` and `settings.api_key.help`. For example, *su* becomes *tu* and *Conecte* becomes *Conecta*.

**Placeholders**
- All 4 are kept exactly as written: `{{count}}` (×2), `{{seconds}}`, `{{email}}`. None were translated or moved somewhere that breaks the grammar.

**Length (button limit is 25 characters)**
| Key | EN | ES |
|---|---|---|
| button.save | 12 | 15 |
| button.delete | 6 | 8 |
| button.cancel | 6 | 8 |
| button.invite | 15 | 20 |
| confirm.delete.title | 30 | 34 (with the placeholder) |

- All buttons are under 25 characters.
- The confirmation title is about 13% longer than the English, which is normal for Spanish. "Integraciones" can't be shortened without losing clarity.
- For `button.invite`, I chose *miembro* for "teammate" because it matches the workspace/member model. *Invitar a un miembro del equipo* (31) was too long.

**Terminology choices**
- **rate limit → *límite de solicitudes*.** The literal *límite de tasa* is a calque (word-for-word copy) that sounds odd to LatAm developers. *Límite de solicitudes* says the same thing clearly.
- **Kept in English:** *endpoint*, *webhook*, *stack*, *API* and the header name *Authorization*. LatAm developers use these words as-is. Translated versions (*punto de conexión*, *pila tecnológica*) sound like machine translation to this audience. *Authorization* has to stay literal because it's the actual HTTP header name.
- **API key → *Clave de API*.** This is the standard term (Stripe, Google Cloud and AWS es-419 all use it).
- **workspace → *espacio de trabajo*.** If the product UI uses "Workspace" as a branded feature name, keep it in English for consistency.
- **within 5 seconds → *en un máximo de 5 segundos*.** This keeps the deadline meaning clearer than *dentro de 5 segundos*.
- **API is feminine** (*su primera API*), which is the standard usage.

**Issues for engineering (they affect the English too)**
1. **Plurals:** `confirm.delete.title`, `confirm.delete.body` and `error.rate_limit` are written only in the plural. With `count = 1` you'd get *"¿Eliminar 1 integraciones?"*. I recommend splitting them into plural keys: `_one`/`_other` if you use i18next, or ICU `{count, plural, one {…} other {…}}`. For example:
   - `confirm.delete.title_one`: `"¿Eliminar {{count}} integración?"`
   - `confirm.delete.title_other`: `"¿Eliminar {{count}} integraciones?"`
2. **Number formatting:** if `{{count}}` or `{{seconds}}` can go above 999, format them by locale. es-419 usually writes `1,000` (Mexico) but some countries write `1.000`. The neutral choice is to use `Intl.NumberFormat('es-419')`.

**Items to check in context**
- `toast.invite_sent`: *Invitación enviada* is masculine/feminine-safe and doesn't depend on who was invited.
- `empty_state.title` is 24 characters. Check it in the empty-state layout if that container has a fixed width.

## Translated JSON (es-419)

```json
{
  "button.save": "Guardar cambios",
  "button.delete": "Eliminar",
  "button.cancel": "Cancelar",
  "button.invite": "Invitar a un miembro",
  "confirm.delete.title": "¿Eliminar {{count}} integraciones?",
  "confirm.delete.body": "Se eliminarán de forma permanente {{count}} integraciones de su espacio de trabajo. Esta acción no se puede deshacer.",
  "error.rate_limit": "Se alcanzó el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {{seconds}} segundos.",
  "empty_state.title": "Aún no hay integraciones",
  "empty_state.body": "Conecte su primera API para comenzar a sincronizar datos en todo su stack tecnológico.",
  "toast.invite_sent": "Invitación enviada a {{email}}",
  "webhook.help": "Su endpoint de webhook debe devolver un código de estado 2xx en un plazo de 5 segundos.",
  "settings.api_key.label": "Clave de API",
  "settings.api_key.help": "Use esta clave en el encabezado Authorization de sus solicitudes."
}
```

## Translator's report

**Overall approach**
- **Formality:** I used *usted* throughout ("Conecte", "Use", "su espacio de trabajo"). It matches a formal, clinical voice and is safe across Latin American markets. If you'd rather sound closer to a modern dev-tool brand, *tú* would also work, but use one form consistently across the product.
- **Neutral es-419:** I left out Spain-specific forms (*vosotros*, "ordenador", "fichero") and strong regional slang. I used the preterite "Se alcanzó" rather than the present perfect, since it reads more naturally across Latin America.
- **Casing:** Spanish UI copy uses sentence case, so "Guardar cambios" has a lowercase *c*. The English source already uses sentence case, so the two match.

**Character budget (buttons, max 25)**

| Key | English | Spanish | Chars |
|---|---|---|---|
| button.save | Save changes (12) | Guardar cambios | 15 ✅ |
| button.delete | Delete (6) | Eliminar | 8 ✅ |
| button.cancel | Cancel (6) | Cancelar | 8 ✅ |
| button.invite | Invite teammate (15) | Invitar a un miembro | 20 ✅ |
| confirm.delete.title | Delete {{count}} integrations? | ¿Eliminar {{count}} integraciones? | about 27 when count is 2 digits ✅ |

**Key decisions**
- **"teammate" → "un miembro":** "Invitar miembro del equipo" is 26 characters, which is over budget. "Compañero" is gendered (*compañero/a*). "Miembro" is gender-neutral in practice and fits easily. The team context comes from the surrounding UI.
- **"Delete" → "Eliminar":** This is the standard term for destructive actions in es-419 software. "Borrar" sounds more casual and suits text editing better.
- **"rate limit" → "límite de solicitudes":** This is clearer than the calque "límite de tasa". I made the sentence impersonal ("Se alcanzó…") so it doesn't sound like it's blaming the user, which fits the clinical tone.
- **"stack" → "stack tecnológico":** Latin American developers use "stack" every day. Adding "tecnológico" keeps the meaning clear without sounding informal.
- **Left in English on purpose:** `endpoint`, `webhook`, `API`, `2xx`, and the header name `Authorization`. These are standard terms in Latin American engineering, and `Authorization` is a literal HTTP header name that must not be translated.
- **"API key" → "Clave de API":** This is the term most large developer platforms use in Spanish.

**Placeholders:** `{{count}}`, `{{seconds}}`, and `{{email}}` are all kept exactly as written, and none of them is wrapped in text that would break interpolation.

**⚠  Issue to fix in the source: plurals**
Both `confirm.delete.*` strings, and `error.rate_limit` with `{{seconds}}`, have no singular form. When `count = 1`, the UI shows "¿Eliminar 1 integraciones?", and the English source has the same bug ("Delete 1 integrations?"). Spanish also needs the noun and verb to agree in number ("Se eliminará… 1 integración" vs. "Se eliminarán… 2 integraciones"). If you use i18next, I recommend adding plural keys:

```json
{
  "confirm.delete.title_one": "¿Eliminar {{count}} integración?",
  "confirm.delete.title_other": "¿Eliminar {{count}} integraciones?",
  "confirm.delete.body_one": "Se eliminará de forma permanente {{count}} integración de su espacio de trabajo. Esta acción no se puede deshacer.",
  "confirm.delete.body_other": "Se eliminarán de forma permanente {{count}} integraciones de su espacio de trabajo. Esta acción no se puede deshacer.",
  "error.rate_limit_one": "Se alcanzó el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {{count}} segundo.",
  "error.rate_limit_other": "Se alcanzó el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {{count}} segundos."
}
```

(i18next decides between singular and plural using `count`, so the rate-limit key would have to take `count` instead of `seconds`. If you'd rather not change that call, you can leave `error.rate_limit` as it is, since a 1-second wait is unlikely.)

**Should be checked by a native-speaking reviewer before release:** the choice between *usted* and *tú* for your brand, and whether "miembro" or another word should be your standard term for teammates across the product.
✓ pass limits-and-plurals

- **Neutral es-419:** I left out Spain-specific forms (*vosotros*, "ordenador", "fichero") and strong regional slang. I used the preterite "Se alcanzó" rather than the present perfect, since it reads more naturally across Latin America.
- **Casing:** Spanish UI copy uses sentence case, so "Guardar cambios" has a lowercase *c*. The English source already uses sentence case, so the two match.

**Character budget (buttons, max 25)**

| Key | English | Spanish | Chars |
|---|---|---|---|
| button.save | Save changes (12) | Guardar cambios | 15 ✅ |
| button.delete | Delete (6) | Eliminar | 8 ✅ |
| button.cancel | Cancel (6) | Cancelar | 8 ✅ |
| button.invite | Invite teammate (15) | Invitar a un miembro | 20 ✅ |
| confirm.delete.title | Delete {{count}} integrations? | ¿Eliminar {{count}} integraciones? | about 27 when count is 2 digits ✅ |

**Key decisions**
- **"teammate" → "un miembro":** "Invitar miembro del equipo" is 26 characters, which is over budget. "Compañero" is gendered (*compañero/a*). "Miembro" is gender-neutral in practice and fits easily. The team context comes from the surrounding UI.
- **"Delete" → "Eliminar":** This is the standard term for destructive actions in es-419 software. "Borrar" sounds more casual and suits text editing better.
- **"rate limit" → "límite de solicitudes":** This is clearer than the calque "límite de tasa". I made the sentence impersonal ("Se alcanzó…") so it doesn't sound like it's blaming the user, which fits the clinical tone.
- **"stack" → "stack tecnológico":** Latin American developers use "stack" every day. Adding "tecnológico" keeps the meaning clear without sounding informal.
- **Left in English on purpose:** `endpoint`, `webhook`, `API`, `2xx`, and the header name `Authorization`. These are standard terms in Latin American engineering, and `Authorization` is a literal HTTP header name that must not be translated.
- **"API key" → "Clave de API":** This is the term most large developer platforms use in Spanish.

**Placeholders:** `{{count}}`, `{{seconds}}`, and `{{email}}` are all kept exactly as written, and none of them is wrapped in text that would break interpolation.

**⚠  Issue to fix in the source: plurals**
Both `confirm.delete.*` strings, and `error.rate_limit` with `{{seconds}}`, have no singular form. When `count = 1`, the UI shows "¿Eliminar 1 integraciones?", and the English source has the same bug ("Delete 1 integrations?"). Spanish also needs the noun and verb to agree in number ("Se eliminará… 1 integración" vs. "Se eliminarán… 2 integraciones"). If you use i18next, I recommend adding plural keys:

```json
{
  "confirm.delete.title_one": "¿Eliminar {{count}} integración?",
  "confirm.delete.title_other": "¿Eliminar {{count}} integraciones?",
  "confirm.delete.body_one": "Se eliminará de forma permanente {{count}} integración de su espacio de trabajo. Esta acción no se puede deshacer.",
  "confirm.delete.body_other": "Se eliminarán de forma permanente {{count}} integraciones de su espacio de trabajo. Esta acción no se puede deshacer.",
  "error.rate_limit_one": "Se alcanzó el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {{count}} segundo.",
  "error.rate_limit_other": "Se alcanzó el límite de solicitudes. Vuelva a intentarlo en {{count}} segundos."
}
```

(i18next decides between singular and plural using `count`, so the rate-limit key would have to take `count` instead of `seconds`. If you'd rather not change that call, you can leave `error.rate_limit` as it is, since a 1-second wait is unlikely.)

**Should be checked by a native-speaking reviewer before release:** the choice between *usted* and *tú* for your brand, and whether "miembro" or another word should be your standard term for teammates across the product.
